. घरेलू उद्योग ने आगे उसी उत्पाद और मलेशियाई निर्यातकों के संबंध में संयुक्त राज्य 


अमेरिका के वाणिज्य विभाग के निर्धारण की ओर ध्यान आकर्षित किया है, जिसमें 
जिनजिंग मलेशिया और ज़िनयी मलेशिया की कॉर्पोरेट संरचनाओं में परस्पर-स्वामित्व वाली 
संस्थाओं की पहचान की गई थी। घरेलू उद्योग ने स्पष्ट किया है कि वह अमेरिकी 
प्राधिकरण द्वारा निर्धारित सब्सिडी दरों को यांत्रिक रूप से अपनाने की माँग नहीं करता, 
किंतु अनुरोध करता है कि वह निर्धारण स्वतंत्र रूप से समूह-स्तर की जाँच की 
प्रासंगिकता को प्रदर्शित करता है और वर्तमान जाँच में संबंधित और परस्पर-स्वामित्व 
वाली संस्थाओं के पूर्ण प्रकटीकरण की आवश्यकता को सुदृढ़ करता है। 


. कि सत्यापन किसी मूलतः अपूर्ण उत्तर की पूर्ति नहीं कर सकता, क्‍योंकि सत्यापन का 


उद्देश्य प्रतिवादी द्वारा प्रकट की गई जानकारी को सत्यापित करना है और यह उन 
संस्थाओं और लेन-देन के प्रकटीकरण का स्थान नहीं ले सकता जिन्हें प्रतिवादी अपने उत्तर 
में पहचानने में विफल रहा। इसलिए घरेलू उद्योग ने अनुरोध किया है कि जहाँ तक 
आवश्यक जानकारी अप्रकट रहती है, प्राधिकारी निर्यातकों को आरोपणीय सब्सिडी का 
निर्धारण नियम 7(8) के अंतर्गत उपलब्ध तथ्यों के आधार पर करे, जिसमें संबंधित 
अथवा परस्पर-स्वामित्व वाली संस्थाओं द्वारा प्राप्त लाभों का उचित आरोपण शामिल है। 


. कि मलेशिया सरकार ने प्राधिकारी द्वारा माँगी गई कार्यक्रम-वार जानकारी उपलब्ध नहीं 


कराई है, जिसमें अमेरिकी प्राधिकरण द्वारा प्रतिकारित सब्सिडी कार्यक्रमों तथा वर्तमान

नामित प्रत्येक संस्था पर यह कसौटी लागू की है। जिनजिंग: उसकी आयकर विवरणी सहित उसके

कारण हुई है। अन्य ज्ञात कारकों के क्षतिकारक प्रभावों को, विशेष रूप से घरेलू उद्योग 
के क्षमता विस्तार का उसकी बिक्री लागत पर प्रभाव, पृथक और विभेदित किया गया है 
और उन्हें सब्सिडीयुक्त आयातों पर आरोपित नहीं किया गया है। 


ix. प्राधिकारी संतुष्ट है कि सब्सिडी और उसके कारण हुई क्षति को प्रतिसंतुलित करने के लिए 
मलेशिया के मूल की अथवा वहाँ से निर्यात की गई संबद्ध वस्तुओं के आयात पर, नीचे 
खंड न में निर्धारित रूप और रीति से, निश्चयात्मक प्रतिकारी शुल्क लगाया जाना 
आवश्यक है। 


न. सिफारिशें 

99. प्राधिकारी नोट करता है कि जाँच आरंभ की गई थी और मलेशिया सरकार सहित सभी 
हितबद्ध पक्षकारों को अधिसूचित की गई थी तथा सब्सिडी, क्षति और कार्य-कारण संबंध के पक्ष 
अथवा विपक्ष में जानकारी/साक्ष्य प्रदान करने का पर्याप्त अवसर दिया गया am नियमों में 
निर्धारित प्रावधानों के अनुसार सब्सिडी, क्षति और कार्य-कारण संबंध की जाँच आरंभ और 
संचालित करने के बाद तथा ऐसे सब्सिडीयुक्त आयातों के कारण घरेलू उद्योग को हुई सारभूत 
क्षति के साथ-साथ धनात्मक सब्सिडी मार्जिन स्थापित करने के बाद, प्राधिकारी का मत है कि 
सब्सिडीकरण और क्षति की भरपाई के लिए अंतिम प्रतिकारी शुल्क लगाना आवश्यक है। अतः 
प्राधिकारी नीचे वर्णित रूप और तरीके से मलेशिया से संबद्ध वस्तुओं के आयात पर अंतिम 
प्रतिकारी शुल्क लगाने की सिफारिश करना आवश्यक समझता है। 


200. नियमों के नियम i9 के अनुसार, और अपनाए गए न्यूनतर शुल्क नियम को ध्यान में 
रखते हुए, नामित प्राधिकारी घरेलू उद्योग को हुई सारभूत क्षति को दूर करने के लिए मलेशिया 
के मूल की अथवा वहाँ से निर्यात की गई संबद्ध वस्तुओं के सभी आयातों पर सब्सिडी मार्जिन 
और क्षति मार्जिन में से न्‍्यूनतर के बराबर सब्सिडी-रोधी शुल्क लगाने की सिफारिश करता है। 


तदनुसार, उपर्युक्त उपबंध द्वारा प्रदत्त शक्तियों के अंतर्गत, प्राधिकारी मलेशिया से संबद्ध वस्तुओं 
के आयात पर, केंद्र सरकार द्वारा आधिकारिक अधिसूचना के प्रकाशन की तिथि से पाँच (5)

MINISTRY OF COMMERCE AND INDUSTRY 
(Department of Commerce) 
(DIRECTORATE GENERAL OF TRADE REMEDIES) 
FINAL FINDINGS 
New Delhi, the 28th September, 2026 
Case No. - CVD(OD — 05/2025 


Subject: Countervailing / Anti-subsidy investigation concerning imports of Clear Float 
Glass originating in or exported from Indonesia and Malaysia. 


A. BACKGROUND OF THE CASE 


F. No. 6/36/2025-DGTR.— Having regard to the Customs Tariff Act, |975 as amended 
from time to time (hereinafter referred to as “the Act’) and the Customs Tariff (Identification, 
Assessment and Collection of Countervailing Duty on Subsidized Articles and for Determination 
of Injury) Rules, 995, as amended from time to time (hereinafter referred as the “CVD Rules” 
or “the Rules’); 


. M/s Gold Plus Float Glass Pvt. Ltd., M/s Gold Plus Glass Industry Ltd., M/s Saint- 
Gobain India Pvt. Ltd. and M/s Sisecam Flat Glass India Ltd. (hereinafter also referred to as “the 
Applicants” or “the Domestic Industry” or “the DI’) had filed an application before the 
Designated Authority (hereinafter also referred to as “the Authority”) in accordance with the 
Customs Tariff Act, |975 (hereinafter also referred to as “the Act”) and the Countervailing Duty 
Rules, as amended from time to time, for imposition of countervailing / anti-subsidy duty on 
imports of “Clear Float Glass” (hereinafter also referred to as “the subject goods’”’) originating in 
or exported from Indonesia and Malaysia (hereinafter also referred to as “the subject countries”). 


2 The Authority, on the basis of sufficient evidence submitted by the Applicants, issued 
Notification No. 6/36/2025-DGTR dated 29.09.2025, published in the Gazette of India 
Extraordinary, initiating the subject investigation in accordance with Rule 6 of the above Rules, 
to determine the existence, degree and effect of the alleged subsidization of the subject goods 
originating in or exported from the subject countries, and to recommend the amount of anti- 
subsidy duty which, if levied, would be adequate to remove the alleged injury to the domestic 
industry. 


B. PROCEDURE 


3. The procedure described herein below has been followed by the Authority with regard to 
the subject investigation: 
i. The Authority received a written application from the Applicants on behalf of the 
domestic industry, alleging subsidisation of Clear Float Glass from Indonesia and 
Malaysia.

The Authority notified the Government of Malaysia and the Government of Indonesia, 
through their missions in India, of the receipt of the anti-subsidy application before 
proceeding to initiate the investigation, in accordance with sub-rule (5) of Rule 6 of the 
Rules, and invited both Governments for consultations with the aim of clarifying the 
situation and arriving at a mutually agreed solution in accordance with Article 3 of the 
SCM Agreement. Consultations were held on 09.09.2025 with the representatives of the 
Government of Malaysia and on 2.09.2025 with the representatives of the Government 
of Indonesia. Both Governments were furnished with the non-confidential version of the 
application setting out the programmes alleged and were afforded an opportunity to 
comment on each. No mutually agreed solution was reached with either Government, 
whether generally or in respect of any individual programme. The written submission of 
the Government of Malaysia on consultation under Article 3.l and the consultation 
paper of the Government of Indonesia dated 2.09.2025 have been taken on record and 
the contentions raised therein have been addressed programme-wise in relevant Section. 


The Authority issued a notification dated 29.09.2025 published in the Gazette of India 
Extraordinary, initiating the anti-subsidy investigation concerning imports of the subject 
goods from Indonesia and Malaysia. 


A copy of the public notice was forwarded by the Authority to all known exporters of the 
subject goods, to the Governments of the subject countries through their missions in 
India, and to other interested parties, in accordance with Rule 7(2) of the Rules. 


The Authority provided a copy of the non-confidential version of the application to the 
known producers/exporters, to the Governments of the subject countries and to other 
interested parties who made a request therefore in writing, in accordance with Rule 7(3) 
of the Rules. ] 


The Authority sent the Exporter’s Questionnaire to the following known 
producers/exporters to elicit relevant information in accordance with the Rules: 


a. PT Mulia Industrindo Tbk, Indonesia 
b. Kibing Group (M) Sdn. Bhd., Malaysia 
c. Xinyi Energy Smart (Malaysia) Sdn. Bhd., Malaysia 


The Governments of the subject countries, through their missions in India, were also 
requested to advise the exporters and producers in their country to respond to the 
questionnaire within the prescribed time limit. A copy of the letter and questionnaire sent 
to the known producers/exporters was also sent to the missions along with the names and 
addresses of the known producers/exporters.

The Authority notes that Jinjing Technology Malaysia Sdn. Bhd. filed its response 
voluntarily; no Exporter’s Questionnaire having been issued to it. Its response being 
complete and having been examined and verified, it has been treated as a cooperating 
producer/exporter and an individual subsidy margin has been determined for it. 

Qingdao Rexi is a trader and not a producer of the subject goods. Its response has not 
been relied upon for the determination of an individual subsidy margin, and exports made 
through it attract the rate applicable to the producer of the goods, subject to the conditions 
set out in Section S. 


The Authority forwarded a copy of the public notice along with the Importer’s 
Questionnaire to 35 known importers, users and user associations of the subject goods in 
India, and to the All India Flat Glass Manufacturers Association, ASSOCHAM, FICCI 
and CII, advising them to make their views known in writing within the time limit 
prescribed, in accordance with Rule 7(4) of the Rules. None of the importers or users 
filed any response, submission or representation. 


The Authority made available the non-confidential versions of the evidence presented by 
the interested parties in the form of a public file kept open for inspection by the interested 
parties. 


A request was made to the Directorate General of Commercial Intelligence and Statistics 
and to DG-Systems, Central Board of Indirect Taxes and Customs, to provide 
transaction-wise details of imports of the subject goods for the injury period. The 
Authority has relied upon DG-Systems data for computation of the volume and value of 
imports, to correlate the quantum of exports from specified exporters and to validate the 
responses filed, to the extent feasible. 


The Authority has examined the import data submitted by the exporters/producers from 
Indonesia and has corroborated the same with the import data obtained from DG 
Systems. Based on the data so examined and corroborated, the Authority has found that 
the volume of imports of the subject goods from Indonesia during the period of 
investigation was less than 4% of the total imports of the subject goods. 


[भाग I—ave I] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 73 


xv. In terms of Rule 6()(d) of the Customs Tariff (Identification, Assessment and 


XV 


ie 


Collection of Countervailing Duty on Subsidized Articles and for Determination of 
Injury) Rules, 995, the Authority is required to terminate an investigation immediately 
where, in the case of a product originating in a developing country, the volume of the 
subsidised imports represents less than four per cent of the total imports of the like 
product into India, unless imports from developing countries whose individual shares are 
each below four per cent collectively account for more than nine per cent of such total 
imports. The individual share of Indonesia and the collective share of all such countries 
have both been examined, and both fall below the respective thresholds, for the reasons 
recorded in relevant section under the heading of negligibility of import volumes. 
Accordingly, having regard to the import volume established on the basis of the verified 
data, the investigation in respect of Indonesia is terminated under Rule 6()(d). In view 
thereof, the Authority has not proceeded to examine the existence of subsidisation in 
Indonesia and has not determined any subsidy margin in respect of that country, 
termination under Rule 6()(d) operating upon the volume of imports and not upon the 
amount of subsidization. 


. The Authority carried out desk verification of the responses filed by the Governments of 


the subject countries and by each of the participating producers/exporters, examining the 
responses against the supporting records furnished, including audited financial 
statements, tax returns and assessments, customs entries, utility invoices, land 
agreements, approval letters and subsidy calculation sheets. On completion of desk 
verification, the Authority sought further clarifications and additional information from 
the participating producers/exporters, inter alia in respect of subsidy programmes 
countervailed by other authorities in respect of the subject goods and the reasons 
advanced for their non-applicability, policy lending, land, exemptions from import duties 
and sales taxes, the provision of electricity, investment-linked tax allowances, and 
benefits received through related and cross-owned entities. The responses furnished have 
been examined and are addressed in relevant Section. 


xvii. The Non-Injurious Price based on the optimum cost of production and cost to make and 


sell the subject goods in India, based on the information furnished by the domestic 
industry on the basis of Generally Accepted Accounting Principles, has been worked out 
so as to ascertain whether anti-subsidy duty lower than the subsidy margin would be 
sufficient to remove injury to the domestic industry. 


XVIil. The period of investigation (POI) for the purpose of the present investigation is 


from Ist April 2024 to 3lst March 2025 (l2 months). The injury investigation period 
covers the data of the previous three years, i.e. April 202] to March 2022, April 2022 to 
March 2023, April 2023 to March 2024, and the POI.

The submissions made by the interested parties during the course of this investigation, to 
the extent supported with evidence and considered relevant to the present investigation, 
have been appropriately considered in this final finding. 


Information provided by the interested parties on a confidential basis was examined with 
regard to the sufficiency of the confidentiality claim. On being satisfied, the 
confidentiality claims have been accepted wherever warranted and such information has 
been considered as confidential and not disclosed to other interested parties. Wherever 
possible, parties providing information on confidential basis were directed to provide 
sufficient non-confidential version of the information filed on confidential basis. 


In accordance with Rule 7(6) of the Rules, the Authority also provided an opportunity to 
all interested parties to present their views orally in a hearing held on 08.05.2026. All the 
parties who attended and presented their views in the oral hearing were requested to file 
written submissions of the views expressed orally. The parties were also provided an 
opportunity to submit their rejoinders thereafter. The submissions made by the interested 
parties during the course of this investigation, wherever found relevant, have been 
addressed in this final finding. 


Wherever an interested party has refused access to, or has otherwise not provided 
necessary information during the course of the present investigation, or has significantly 
impeded the investigation, such parties have been considered as non-cooperative and the 
final finding has been recorded on the basis of the facts available. 


The examination and verification of the information submitted by the cooperating 
producers/exporters from the subject countries were also carried out to the extent deemed 
necessary and the same has been relied upon for the purpose of the present final findings. 


A disclosure statement containing the essential facts of the investigation which have 
formed the basis of the final findings was issued to the interested parties on i7" 
September 2026 and the interested parties were afforded an opportunity to furnish their 
comments thereon by 24.09.2026. Post-disclosure comments were received from the 
domestic industry and from the other interested parties, as recorded in Section Q below. 
The submissions made by the interested parties, arguments raised, and the comments to 
disclosure statement received from the interested parties have been considered, to the 
extent found relevant, non-repetitive and supported with evidence in this final finding 
notification.

xxvi. The Exchange rate adopted is US$ | = हें 85.43. It is the simple average of the rates 
notified by the Central Board of Indirect Taxes and Customs under Section 4 of the 
Customs Act, 962 for the conversion of the value of imported goods, taking the rates in 
force in each of the twelve months of the period of investigation (Source: 
https://foservices.icegate. gov.in/#/services/viewExchangeRate). 


xxvii.Currency of the exporting country to the United States dollar. The amount of a 
subsidy is first measured in the currency in which the benefit arose and the denominator 
is expressed. Where a conversion into United States dollars was required for that purpose, 
the Authority has applied the rate used by the responding producer or exporter itself in its 
own questionnaire response for the period of investigation, that rate being drawn from the 
respondent’s own books and supporting records. The rate so applied in each case is 
recorded in the subsidy determination for that respondent. 


C. PRODUCT UNDER CONSIDERATION AND LIKE ARTICLE 
4. At the stage of initiation, the product under consideration was defined as under: 


The product under consideration in the present investigation is “Clear Float Glass of 
nominal thicknesses ranging from 4 mm to l2 mm (both inclusive)”, the nominal 
thickness being as per BIS 4900:2000, originating in or exported from Indonesia and 
Malaysia. 


C.l Submissions made by the producers / exporters / importers / other interested parties 


5. No submission has been made by the producers/exporters or by any other interested party 
with regard to the scope of the product under consideration and like article. 


C.2 Views of the Domestic Industry 


6. The submissions of the domestic industry with regard to the product under consideration 
and like article are as follows: 


i. The product under consideration is “Clear Float Glass of nominal thicknesses ranging from 
4 mm to |2 mm (both inclusive)”, the nominal thickness being as per BIS 4900:2000 (as 
amended). 


ii. Clear Float Glass is used in construction, refrigeration, mirror and solar energy industries. 
The product is a superior quality of glass. Due to its inherent strength, high optical clarity 
and distortion-free smooth surface, the applications of the product have been increasing 
for different purposes. 


iii. Float Glass is classified under Chapter Heading 70 “Glass and glassware”. The subject 
goods are also imported under tariff headings 7003, 7004, 7005, 7009, 70I3, 70I5, 706, 
708, 70l9 and 7020. The customs classification is indicative only and is in no way 
binding upon the product scope. 


76 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.!] 


iv. There is no known difference in the subject goods produced by the domestic industry and 
those imported from the subject countries. The two are comparable in terms of physical 
and chemical characteristics, manufacturing process and technology, functions and uses, 
product specifications, distribution and marketing, and tariff classification, and are 
technically and commercially substitutable. They should therefore be treated as “like 
article” under the Rules. 


C.3 Examination by the Authority 


7; The product under consideration for the purpose of the present investigation is “Clear 
Float Glass of nominal thicknesses ranging from 4 mm to |2 mm (both inclusive)”, the nominal 
thickness being as per BIS 4900:2000 (as amended). 


8. Clear Float Glass is used in construction, refrigeration, mirror and solar energy industries 
and is classified under Chapter Heading 70 “Glass and glassware”. The classification at the 8- 
digit level is 7005090, even though the subject goods are also classified and imported under the 
sub-headings referred to above. The customs classification is indicative only and is in no way 
binding upon the product scope. 


9. In order to give the scope precision and to guard against avoidance of the measure, the 
Authority considers it appropriate to record the following clarifications, which do not expand the 
product scope as notified at initiation: 


i. The subject goods are articles of soda-lime-silica glass manufactured by floating a 
continuous ribbon of molten glass over a bath of molten tin or other liquid metal of 
greater density than molten glass, annealing the glass in a lehr, and cutting it to 
dimension. 


ul. The country of origin of the subject goods is the country in which the soda-lime-silica 
glass is first manufactured by the float process and annealed, irrespective of the location 
of any subsequent downstream finishing or fabrication. 


Cutting, edging, bevelling, drilling, polishing, grinding, washing and packing do not 
remove the goods from the scope, provided that the glass as first floated satisfied the 
description of the product under consideration. 


ne 


ili. 


iv. The written description of the product under consideration, as clarified above, is 
dispositive. 


0._ The expression “like article” is not defined in the Rules. The Authority has applied the 
meaning uniformly given to that expression in the determination of injury, namely an article 
which is identical or alike in all respects to the article under investigation or, in the absence of 
such an article, another article which although not alike in all respects has characteristics closely 
resembling those of the article under investigation. 


ll. It is noted that there is no known difference in the subject goods produced by the Indian 
industry and the product under consideration exported from the subject countries. The subject 
goods produced by the Indian industry and those imported from Indonesia and Malaysia are 
comparable in terms of physical characteristics, manufacturing process and technology, functions 
and uses, product specifications, pricing, distribution and marketing and tariff classification of 


[भाग L—ave |] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 77 


the goods. The two are technically and commercially substitutable. The subject goods produced 
by the domestic industry are like article to the product under consideration imported from 
Indonesia and Malaysia within the scope and meaning of Rule 2(d) of the Rules. 


IBS No other argument has been made by any interested party on the product under 
consideration and like the article. It is therefore confirmed that the scope of the product under 
consideration in the present investigation remains the same as mentioned in the initiation 
notification, subject to the clarifications recorded above. 


D. DOMESTIC INDUSTRY AND STANDING 
D.l Submissions made on behalf of the opposing interested parties 


3. The other interested parties have not made any submission with regard to the scope of the 
domestic industry and standing. 


D.2 Submissions made on behalf of the Domestic Industry 


4. The submissions of the domestic industry with regard to the scope of the domestic 
industry and standing are as follows: 


i. The present application has been filed by M/s Gold Plus Float Glass Pvt. Ltd., M/s Gold 
Plus Glass Industry Ltd., M/s Saint-Gobain India Pvt. Ltd. and M/s Sisecam Flat Glass 
India Ltd., who collectively command 85% share in Indian production of the subject 
goods during the period of investigation. 


il. There are only two other known producers of the product under consideration in the 
country, namely M/s Asahi India Glass Ltd. and M/s Gujarat Guardian Ltd. Neither has 
supported nor opposed the present investigation. 


ili. The domestic industry has not imported the subject goods from the subject countries and 
is not related to any exporter of the subject goods in the subject countries or to any 
importer of the subject goods in India. 


D.3 Examination by the Authority 


5. Rule 2(b) of the Rules defines the domestic industry as under: 


“(b) “domestic industry’ means the domestic producers as a whole engaged in the 
manufacture of the like article and any activity connected therewith or those whose 
collective output of the said article constitutes a major proportion of the total domestic 
production of that article except when such producers are related to the exporters or 
importers of the alleged subsidized article or are themselves importers thereof in which 
case the term ‘domestic industry’ may be construed as referring to the rest of the 
producers.” 


6. As per the available information, the present application has been filed by M/s Gold Plus 
Float Glass Pvt. Ltd., M/s Gold Plus Glass Industry Ltd., M/s Saint-Gobain India Pvt. Ltd. and 
M/s Sisecam Flat Glass India Ltd., who collectively command 85% share in Indian production 
of the subject goods during the period of investigation, and who therefore account for a major 
proportion of Indian production. It is further noted that the Applicants have neither imported the 


78 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.]] 


subject goods from the subject countries nor are they related to any producer/exporter of the 
subject goods in Indonesia and Malaysia or to any importer in India. 


ee 


In view of the above, and since none of the interested parties has made any submission in 


relation to the standing of the domestic industry, the Authority holds that the Applicants 
constitute the domestic industry within the meaning of Rule 2(b) of the Rules, and considers that 
the application satisfies the criteria of standing in terms of Rule 6(3) of the Rules. 


E. CONFIDENTIALITY 


E.l Submissions made on behalf of the opposing interested parties 


8. 


The other interested parties have made the following submissions: 


i. That the responses filed by them are as per the Trade Notice, and that the non-confidential 


versions have been filed as per the practice of the Authority. The allegation of the 
domestic industry that they have not filed proper non-confidential versions is therefore 
incorrect. 


ii. That the domestic industry has withheld actual or even aggregated figures for several key 


injury parameters while disclosing only indexed trends. Such disclosure is insufficient to 
permit meaningful defence of the respondents’ interests. Aggregate information relating 
to four domestic producers could have been disclosed without revealing business 
confidential information. The excessive confidentiality claimed is inconsistent with 
established practice, particularly when actual aggregate figures for similar parameters 
were disclosed in the recent anti-dumping sunset review concerning the same product. 


ui. That confidentiality cannot be granted as a matter of course and must be supported by 


good cause. The Authority should direct the domestic industry to provide complete and 
meaningful non-confidential versions of the information relied upon. In the absence of 
adequate disclosure, the confidential information should be disregarded in accordance 
with Rule 8(3) of the Rules. 


E.2 Submissions made by the Domestic Industry 


9. 


The submissions of the domestic industry with regard to confidentiality are as follows: 


i. That the responding exporters and Governments have claimed excessive confidentiality 


over both narrative and quantitative information in their questionnaire responses, 
resulting in non-confidential versions that do not permit a reasonable understanding of 
the information relied upon. 


ii. That the non-confidential versions fail to disclose essential information, including the 


names of subsidy programmes, their nature, operational framework and implementing 
authorities, whether such programmes were availed, the reasons for non-availment, and 
the supporting evidence, thereby preventing meaningful comment. While confidential 
numerical data may be indexed where justified, the existence and essential features of 
subsidy programmes cannot legitimately be treated as confidential. 


ili. That the composition of the domestic industry in the present investigation is materially 


different from that in the earlier anti-dumping sunset review concerning the same 


[भाग I—ave I] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 79 


product. Disclosure of actual aggregate figures would have enabled interested parties, 
including competing domestic producers, to derive or accurately estimate the confidential 
business information of individual producers by comparing the figures disclosed in the 
present investigation with information available from previous proceedings and other 
public sources, causing serious commercial prejudice. The domestic industry has 
accordingly disclosed indexed trends and meaningful non-confidential summaries to the 
maximum extent possible, consistent with Rule 8 of the Rules and Trade Notice 
No. 0/208. 


iv. That where adequate disclosure is not made, the Authority should draw appropriate 
adverse inferences and proceed on the basis of facts available in accordance with Rule 
7(8) of the Rules and Article !2.7 of the SCM Agreement. 


E.3 Examination by the Authority 


20. With regard to confidentiality of information, Rule 8 of the Rules provides for the 
treatment of information supplied on a confidential basis, requires a non-confidential summary in 
sufficient detail to permit a reasonable understanding of the substance of the confidential 
information, and permits the Authority to disregard such information where the request for 
confidentiality is not warranted or the supplier is unwilling to make the information public or to 
authorise its disclosure in a generalized or summary form. 


ak. The Authority notes that the composition of the domestic industry in the present 
investigation differs from that in the earlier anti-dumping sunset review concerning the same 
product. The Authority further notes the contention of the domestic industry that disclosure of 
actual aggregate figures could have enabled interested parties, including competing domestic 
producers, to derive the confidential business information of individual producers by comparing 
such figures with information available from previous proceedings and other public sources. The 
Authority considers that the domestic industry has provided indexed trends and non-confidential 
summaries to the extent feasible while safeguarding confidential business information in 
accordance with Rule 8 of the Rules and Trade Notice No. 0/208. The Authority has examined 
the confidential information and is satisfied that the confidentiality claims are warranted to the 
extent accepted. 


22. As regards the confidentiality claimed by the responding parties, the Authority accepts in 
principle the contention of the domestic industry that the existence, legal basis, administering 
authority and essential operating features of a subsidy programme are not susceptible of 
confidential treatment. The programme-wise examination in the relevant Section of these final 
findings has been framed accordingly, setting out for every programme examined the legal basis, 
the administering authority and the essential features, company-specific quantum alone having 
been treated as confidential where a claim was made and accepted. 


23. Information provided by the interested parties on a confidential basis was examined with 
regard to the sufficiency of the confidentiality claim. On being satisfied, the Authority has 
accepted the confidentiality claims wherever warranted and such information has been 
considered confidential and not disclosed to other interested parties. Business sensitive 
information has been kept confidential as per practice. The Authority notes that any information 
which is available in the public domain cannot be treated as confidential.

F. MISCELLANEOUS SUBMISSIONS AND COOPERATION 


F.l Submissions made on behalf of the Domestic Industry 


24. 


The submissions of the domestic industry are as follows: 


i. That the responses of the participating exporters cannot be accepted as they have not 
provided complete information relating to their related and cross-owned entities in 
Malaysia. In the case of Kibing Group (M), the information of Malaysia Kibing Energy 
Saving Glass Co., Ltd and CS Eco Glass (M) Sdn. Bhd. has not been furnished; and in 
the case of Xinyi Energy Smart (Malaysia), the information of Xinyi Solar (Malaysia) 
Sdn. Bhd. and Xinyi Photovoltaic (Malaysia) Co., Ltd. has not been furnished. Unless the 
data of these entities is examined, the Authority would not be in a position to calculate 
the total subsidy benefit availed by the respective groups. 


u. Jinjing Technology Malaysia Sdn. Bhd. has received an income tax exemption under a 
“5+5” year policy, as disclosed in the annual report of its related company, and the 
Authority should examine the extent and benefit received under such exemption along 
with the relevant approvals, tax filings and accounting records. 


iii. That the exporters have stated “not applicable” in respect of most schemes on the ground 
that they did not avail the specified programmes. However, where a company is eligible 
for a programme there is no reason to believe it would not have benefited under it, and 
the company should either show absence of eligibility or demonstrate why it has not 
availed the benefit available to it. 


iv. That the Government of Malaysia has not provided information relating to the companies 
under investigation as to which companies applied for, claimed, received or used the 
assistance stated, nor information about their related companies and the benefits availed 
by them, nor the information sought at the oral hearing regarding subsidy duties levied by 
other jurisdictions. Such non-submission vitiates the purpose of obtaining a response 
from the Government of the exporting country, and the Authority should apply facts 
available and draw appropriate adverse inferences in accordance with Rule 7(8) of the 
Rules and Article 2.7 of the SCM Agreement. 


v. That the Government of Indonesia has provided details only of subsidies provided to the 
participating exporter, has not provided any clarification relating to direct and indirect 
subsidies provided to other Indonesian producers, and has not provided information 
relating to PT Xinyi Glass Indonesia and PT KCC Glass Indonesia. 


vi. That the Governments of the subject countries and the responding exporters failed to 
provide complete programme-wise disclosures, supporting evidence and concordance 
tables correlating the subsidy programmes examined in other jurisdictions with those 
disclosed before the Authority. 


vii. That the evidentiary standard at the stage of initiation is limited to the information 
reasonably available to the applicant under Articles l].2 and .3 of the SCM 
Agreement, and that the respondents’ contention requiring exporter-specific evidence at 
the initiation stage is inconsistent with the legal framework governing anti-subsidy 
investigations.

viii. That on-site verification of the participating exporters, including verification of the

F.2 Submissions made on behalf of the other interested parties 
29: The submissions of the other interested parties are as follows: 


i. That the application does not satisfy the prima facie evidentiary requirements under 
Articles ].2 and .3 of the SCM Agreement, as several alleged programmes are based 
on generalised government information, secondary sources or speculative assumptions 
without sufficient evidence of financial contribution, benefit, specificity or actual use by 
the responding exporters. Reliance is placed on the WTO Panel Report in China — 
GOES (DS44). 


ii. That mere identification of numerous alleged subsidy programmes does not establish that 
an exporter actually availed such programmes or received a specific and countervailable 
benefit, and any determination should be based on verified company records rather than 
estimates provided by the domestic industry. 


ili. That each cooperating producer/exporter should receive an individual subsidy margin and 
injury margin on the basis of its own verified information, consistent with the established 
practice of the Authority. 


iv. That the newly raised subsidy programmes, including policy lending, provision of land 
and provision of electricity, could not be examined without following the requirements 
under Rules 6 and 6A of the Rules. 


v. That no subsidy, financing, land, electricity concession or other assistance under the 
identified programmes was received directly or indirectly through any related, cross- 
owned or affiliated entity. 


F.3 Examination by the Authority 


26. On the evidentiary standard at initiation. The Authority notes that the evidentiary 
standard at the stage of initiation is limited to the information reasonably available to the 
applicant under Articles l.2 and .3 of the SCM Agreement. An applicant cannot be required 
to furnish exporter-specific evidence of utilisation before the investigation which alone can elicit 
such information. The Authority accordingly does not accept the contention that the present 
investigation was initiated without sufficient evidence. 


27. On individual margins. The Authority accepts the contention that a cooperating 
producer/exporter is entitled to an individual subsidy margin determined on the basis of its own 
verified information rather than on estimates contained in the application. Individual subsidy 
margins have accordingly been determined for Xinyi Energy Smart (Malaysia), Kibing Group 
(M) and Jinjing Technology Malaysia. 


28. On eligibility and receipt. The Authority accepts that where a respondent is eligible 
under a programme, a presumption arises that the programme was available to be availed, and 
that the party asserting non-availment carries the burden of displacing it. The presumption is, 


82 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.]] 


however, one of availability and not of receipt, and it is rebuttable upon the documentary record. 
Where the Government of the exporting country and the respondent have both stated that a 
programme was not availed, and the tax returns, tax assessments, customs entries and audited 
accounts of the respondent have been examined and disclose no corresponding claim or credit, 
the presumption stands displaced and the Authority is satisfied that no benefit was received 
during the period of investigation. The Authority records that this conclusion rests in each case 
upon the respondent’s own tax and customs records examined against its audited accounts, and 
not upon its assertion or upon the statement of its Government alone; that where the records did 
not bear out the assertion the Authority has said so and has countervailed the programme; and 
that the conclusion is confined to the respondent whose records were examined, the position of 
producers and exporters which did not participate being dealt with separately under the head of 
residual subsidy margins. 


29. On the examination of programmes identified during the investigation. The 
Authority does not accept the objection that programmes identified in the course of the 
investigation cannot be examined. An investigating authority is not confined to the programmes 
identified in the application. Where information coming to light in the course of the investigation 
discloses a further programme, the Authority may examine it, provided the affected parties are 
given notice of the allegation, of the essential facts relied upon, and an adequate opportunity to 
defend their interests. That condition has been satisfied: the Authority put the allegations to each 
participating exporter in specific terms following desk verification; each responded 
substantively, furnishing agreements, invoices, approvals and calculations; and the essential 
facts, including the basis of determination, were disclosed to all interested parties in the 
Disclosure Statement dated 7.09.2026, and the parties were afforded an opportunity to comment 
thereon. The income-tax exemption dealt with as Programme 7 was raised by the domestic 
industry in the course of the investigation, and has been examined upon the audited financial 
statements and income-tax computation filed by the respondent itself with its questionnaire 
response. 


30. On the reliability of the response of the Government of Malaysia. The Authority 
notes that the Government of Malaysia stated, in respect of a number of programmes, that none 
of the responding producers/exporters received any benefit during the POI, and that this 
statement is contradicted by the responding exporters themselves in respect of the Investment 
Tax Allowance, the Accelerated Capital Allowance, the Industrial Building Allowance, the 
Allowance for Plant and Machinery, the Sales Tax Exemption and the Buyer Credit Guarantee. 
The Authority further notes that the Reinvestment Allowance is not among the programmes 
disclosed by the Government of Malaysia or alleged in the application, yet was availed by a 
responding exporter and came to light only upon the desk verification. 


3l. Determination on facts available. Rule 7(8) of the Rules provides that where an 
interested party refuses access to, or otherwise does not provide necessary information within a 
reasonable period, or significantly impedes the investigation, the Authority may record its 
findings on the basis of facts available to it. The Authority determines as follows. The statement 
of the Government of Malaysia that no responding producer/exporter received benefit under the 
programmes named above is not accepted, being contradicted by the responding exporters 
themselves, and the Authority has proceeded upon the information furnished and verified in the 
responses of those exporters. No adverse inference is drawn against the responding 
producers/exporters, whose responses were filed in full, were verified against their supporting 


[भाग avs ] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 83 


records and were supplemented upon every query raised. In respect of the Reinvestment 
Allowance, which was disclosed neither by the Government nor in the application, the Authority 
has proceeded upon the amount reported and evidenced by the exporter which availed it. In 
respect of producers and exporters which did not participate, the subsidy margin has been 
determined upon facts available in the manner set out in relevant Section under the head of 
residual subsidy margins. 


32. On related and cross-owned entities. At the stage of desk verification the Authority 
noted that the responding exporters had not furnished complete particulars of their related, 
affiliated and cross-owned entities in Malaysia and of their transactions with them. This was 
recorded in the Disclosure Statement, and each exporter was put on notice to furnish such 
particulars with its comments, failing which the Authority might proceed on the facts available 
under Rule 7(8) of the Rules. The Authority has since examined, exporter by exporter, the post- 
disclosure submissions together with the audited financial statements and income-tax returns on 
the record. Facts available have been applied to the specific items of necessary information not 
furnished, or contradicted by the exporter’s own records; information furnished and verified has 
not been disregarded. The determination in respect of each exporter is recorded in Section Q. 


33. | On the request for on-site verification. The domestic industry has requested that the 
responses of the participating producers/exporters and of the Governments be verified on site. 
The Authority notes that the purpose of verification is to satisfy the Authority of the accuracy of 
the information furnished. In the present investigation the Authority has carried out desk 
verification of every response, examining each response against the audited financial statements, 
tax returns and assessments, customs entries, utility invoices, land agreements, approval letters 
and subsidy calculation sheets furnished in support, and has sought and obtained further 
clarifications and additional documents where the material furnished was incomplete or 
inconsistent. The Authority is satisfied that the information relied upon has been verified to the 
extent necessary for the purposes of this determination, and the request for on-site verification is 
accordingly not acceded to. 


ASSESSMENT OF SUBSIDY — METHODOLOGY, PARAMETERS, 
COUNTERVAILABILITY AND SUBSIDY MARGINS 


G. METHODOLOGY 


34. Section 9 of the Customs Tariff Act, 975 read with the Rules governs the 
determination of a countervailable subsidy. A subsidy is said to exist where there is a financial 
contribution by a government or public body, or income or price support, which confers a benefit 
upon the recipient, and the subsidy is specific within the meaning of Article 2 of the SCM 
Agreement. 


35. Article 4 of the SCM Agreement provides guidelines and methodology for calculating 
the benefit to the recipient and requires that any method used by the investigating authority be 
provided for in national legislation or implementing regulations and that its application in each 
particular case be transparent and adequately explained. In accordance with that requirement, the 
Rules lay down the methodology for determining the quantum of subsidisation, and the 
determination in this investigation is in accordance with those guidelines. 


84 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.!] 


36. The Authority has examined each programme against the following elements in 
sequence: (i) the legal basis and administering authority; () whether a financial contribution by 
a government or public body is established; (॥) whether the programme is specific; (iv) whether 
a benefit is conferred, and the basis on which the benefit has been measured; and (v) the ad 
valorem subsidy rate. Where any of the first three elements is not established, no rate has been 
determined. 


37. Anmnexures to the Rules. The Authority has determined the amount of the 
countervailable subsidy in accordance with Annexure IV to the Rules, which sets out the 
guidelines for the calculation of the amount of subsidy, and has examined whether each 
programme is a subsidy conferred on a limited number of persons in accordance with Annexure 
II to the Rules read with Article 2 of the SCM Agreement. Injury has been determined in 
accordance with Rule |3 of the Rules read with Annexure I to the Rules. 


38. Recurring and non-recurring benefits. A benefit conferred and consumed within the 
year in which it arises has been treated as recurring and expensed to that year. A benefit 
conferred once but enjoyed over the life of the asset which it finances has been treated as non- 
recurring and allocated over the average useful life of !5 years of the renewable physical assets 
used in the production of the subject goods, only the portion attributable to the period of 
investigation having been countervailed. The Authority has applied that treatment to the 
provision of land and to the exemptions from sales tax on equipment and from import duty on 
machinery. In respect of every other programme the Authority has examined the records of the 
respondent concerned and has determined upon that examination whether the benefit is recurring 
or non-recurring, the classification so determined being recorded at the programme concerned. 
The provision of natural gas and electricity, the exemption of import duty and sales tax on raw 
materials and consumables, and income-tax allowances claimed in the year of assessment falling 
within the period of investigation have been treated as recurring. 


39. Denominator. A subsidy which is not tied to a particular product has been allocated over 
the total sales of the recipient; a subsidy tied to a particular product or facility has been allocated 
over the sales of that product or facility. 


40. Computation of the ad valorem subsidy rate. For every programme the rate has been 
computed as a fraction. The numerator is the amount of the benefit attributable to the period of 
investigation. The denominator is the turnover described in the preceding paragraph. The rate is 
the numerator expressed as a percentage of the denominator. 


Al. The numerator is arrived at as follows. Where the benefit is recurring, the whole of the 
benefit arising in the period of investigation is taken. Where the benefit is non-recurring, the 
benefit is first computed in full and then divided by the average useful life of fifteen years, so 
that only one-fifteenth of it is taken into the period of investigation; the Authority has applied 
that treatment to the provision of land and to the exemptions from sales tax on equipment and 
from import duty on machinery. In respect of the remaining programmes the Authority has 
determined the amount attributable to the period of investigation upon the verified records of the 
respondent concerned, and the treatment adopted is recorded at the programme concerned. 
Where the benefit arises in a currency other than that of the denominator, it is converted into the 
currency of the denominator at the rate used by the respondent in its own questionnaire response, 
so that numerator and denominator stand in the same currency; no rate of the Authority’s own 
has been substituted for a rate the respondent has evidenced. 


[भाग [खण्ड ] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 85 


42. The numerator and the denominator for each programme are particulars of the sales, costs 
and tax affairs of the respondent concerned and have been claimed by it as confidential under 
Rule 8 of the Rules. They are accordingly shown in these final findings as “***”. The basis of 
the computation, the benchmark where one is used, the treatment of the benefit as recurring or 
non-recurring, the denominator adopted and the resulting ad valorem rate are set out in each 
case, so that each respondent is able to verify the computation against its own figures and every 
other interested party is able to test the method. 


43. The basis upon which the rates in these final findings have been determined is as follows. 
The Authority has itself determined the rate in respect of two programmes, being the provision of 
land for less than adequate remuneration and the provision of natural gas for less than adequate 
remuneration, the benchmark in each case being that adopted by the Authority for the reasons 
recorded programme-wise below. In respect of every other programme the Authority has 
examined the quantification furnished by the respondent in its subsidy calculation annexure 
against the audited financial statements, tax returns and assessments, customs entries, utility 
invoices and other supporting records of that respondent, and has satisfied itself as to the 
amounts so quantified. Where the Authority is so satisfied, the amount verified has been taken as 
the measure of the benefit, being the verified quantification of the revenue foregone or of the 
advantage conferred, and the Authority has determined the rate accordingly. Where upon 
examination the Authority has found the basis of a quantification to depart from that described 
above, it has recorded the departure at the programme concerned. The basis so adopted, and each 
departure so recorded, was set out in the Disclosure Statement, and the post-disclosure comments 
of the interested parties thereon have been considered at the programme concerned and in 
Section Q below. 


44. Benchmarks. Where a programme consists of the provision of goods by a government, 
the benefit is the difference between the price paid and an appropriate benchmark. Article 4(d) 
of the SCM Agreement establishes a preference for prevailing market conditions in the country 
of provision. Where, however, the government is the predominant provider and the price is 
administered by or on behalf of the government, in-country prices do not reflect market 
conditions and cannot serve as a benchmark, and an out-of-country benchmark is required. The 
benchmark adopted, and the reasons for its adoption, are recorded programme-wise below. 


45. De minimis. A subsidy margin below [% ad valorem is de minimis in terms of Article 
.9 of the SCM Agreement, the corresponding threshold for a developing country Member 
being 2% ad valorem in terms of Article 27.0. 


46. In view of the termination, recorded in Section B above, of the investigation in respect of 
Indonesia upon the ground of negligible import volumes, no examination of the existence of 
subsidisation in Indonesia has been carried out and no subsidy margin has been determined in 
respect of that country. The examination in this Section is accordingly confined to Malaysia. The 
Authority has examined the countervailability of all thirty-two programmes alleged in respect of 
Malaysia, together with the further programmes disclosed by the responding exporters and 
identified by the Authority during and following desk verification. The programmes are dealt 
with under three heads: programmes determined to be countervailable and availed during the 
period of investigation; programmes determined not to be countervailable; and programmes 
determined not to have been used during the period of investigation. 


86 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.!] 


H. MALAYSIA 


H. Programmes determined to be countervailable and availed during the POI 


Programme I: Provision of Land for Less Than Adequate Remuneration 


47. Origin of the allegation. This programme was not alleged in the application and was not 
disclosed by the Government of Malaysia. It was identified by the Authority in the course of 
desk verification, upon which information was sought from the participating exporters as to the 
mode of acquisition of land, the consideration paid, the applicable benchmark or guideline value, 
and the terms of acquisition. 


48. Submissions by the exporters. Jinjing Technology Malaysia submitted that it acquired 
the lease of the land from its Chinese affiliate, which had initially obtained the lease from Kulim 
Technology Park Corporation, a government-owned entity, under an agreement dated 
27.2.207; that no guideline value was available; and that there were no other industrial users in 
the park for comparison. Kibing Group (M) submitted that it purchased land from Samsung 
Malaysia, a private entity, and from Seriemas Development Sdn. Bhd., a government-related 
party; that the purchases were made through arm’s-length negotiations and at fair market value; 
and that the Malaysian taxation authority had confirmed the relevant transfer price as market 
price. Xinyi Energy Smart (Malaysia) submitted that the relevant land parcels were purchased 
directly from Perbadanan Kemajuan Negeri Melaka through private sales; that it could not locate 
the applicable guideline value; and that it could not confirm whether similar price and payment 
terms were available to other industrial users. 


49. Examination by the Authority — financial contribution. The provision of land 
constitutes a financial contribution only where the land is provided by a government or a public 
body. The Authority has examined the position in respect of each parcel: 


i. In the case of Xinyi Energy Smart (Malaysia), the parcels were acquired from a State 
Development Corporation, which is a public body, and the element is satisfied. 


ii. In the case of Kibing Group (M), one parcel was acquired from a government-linked 
entity and one from a party which the respondent describes as a private entity. A purchase 
from a private vendor does not constitute a financial contribution by the Government of 
Malaysia at any price. The Authority has accordingly excluded that parcel from 
consideration and has examined only the parcel acquired from the government-linked 
entity. 


lii. In the case of Jinjing Technology Malaysia, the leasehold interest was originally allotted 
by a government-owned corporation and passed to the respondent from a cross-owned 
affiliate. The Authority determines that the interposition of a cross-owned affiliate does 
not defeat the finding. The land is land in the subject country, allotted by a body of that 
country, in respect of the very site on which the subject goods are produced, and it has at 
all times since allotment been dedicated to that production. To hold otherwise would 
permit the benefit of a government allotment to be insulated from countervailing 
measures by the routing of title through an affiliate. 


50. The Authority records that this determination is to be distinguished from its 
determination on policy lending recorded below. In the case of policy lending, the loans were 


[भाग I—ave I] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 87 


extended by lenders outside the subject country to entities outside the subject country, in respect 
of no asset situated in the subject country, and the subsequent movement of funds between 
affiliates did not convert them into a financial contribution by the Government of Malaysia. In 
the present case the financial contribution was made by a body of the subject country in respect 
of the site of production situated in that country. 


5I. Examination by the Authority — specificity. Land in a designated industrial park is not 
available generally. It is allotted only to enterprises admitted to that park, upon application and 
approval. The programme is accordingly specific within the meaning of Article 2.2 of the SCM 
Agreement, being limited to enterprises located within a designated geographical region, and in 
the alternative under Article 2.l(a), being limited to enterprises approved for admission. 


52. Examination by the Authority — benefit. No guideline value was furnished by any 
respondent, and no respondent has contended that a contemporaneous transaction is available for 
comparison. The Authority has relied upon the Malaysia Industrial Park Directory published by 
the Federation of Malaysian Manufacturers in collaboration with the Malaysian Investment 
Development Authority and relevant stakeholders, which provides information on industrial 
parks including the developer, tenure, selling prices and infrastructure available. The Directory 
was prepared as an investor-oriented reference for assessing and identifying suitable industrial 
locations, is independently published and publicly available, and was prepared with the 
participation of the national investment authority. The Authority accordingly considers the 
selling prices reported therein to be relevant and objective publicly available information 
regarding prevailing industrial land prices in Malaysia, and an appropriate and reliable basis for 
determining the applicable benchmark. 


53. The Authority does not accept the contention that a privately negotiated transaction, or 
the absence of a guideline value, establishes that the consideration paid represents prevailing 
market value. A negotiated price between a state corporation and an applicant enterprise is not, 
without more, a market price; the question under Article l4(d) is whether the remuneration was 
adequate by reference to prevailing market conditions, and not whether the parties negotiated. 
The Authority has therefore compared the consideration actually paid or contracted with the 
applicable benchmark rate for the industrial park concerned. 


54. The Authority further notes that, in the case of Xinyi Energy Smart (Malaysia), the 
information furnished by the respondent itself records that certain of the parcels acquired are not 
used for the production of the subject merchandise. A benefit conferred in respect of land which 
does not support production of the subject goods is not attributable to the subject goods, and 
those parcels have accordingly been excluded. 


55. The acquisition of land is a one-time benefit conferred in respect of an asset used over a 
number of years and has accordingly been treated as non-recurring and allocated over the 
average useful life in accordance with the allocation rule set out in the methodology above, only 
the portion attributable to the period of investigation having been countervailed. 


56. Computation of the benefit. The benefit has been computed parcel by parcel. For each 
qualifying parcel the Authority has taken the area in square feet and multiplied it by the 
benchmark rate per square foot for the industrial park in which that parcel stands, so arriving at 
the benchmark value of the parcel; from that value it has deducted the consideration actually paid 
or contracted by the respondent. The benchmark rate applied is RM 35 per square foot in the 


88 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.]] 


case of each of the three parks concerned, being the rate reported for that park in the Malaysia 
Industrial Park Directory; where the Directory reports a range for a park, the lower end of the 
range has been taken. 


57. The numerator is the aggregate of the amounts so computed for the qualifying parcels of 
the respondent, divided by fifteen, being the average useful life adopted in the methodology 
above. In the case of the leasehold interest held by Jinjing Technology Malaysia the Authority 
has allocated the benefit over the average useful life of fifteen years and not over the sixty-year 
term of the lease, the allocation period under Annexure IV being referable to the useful life of the 
asset employed in production and not to the duration of the title by which it is held. The 
denominator is the total turnover of the respondent in the period of investigation, expressed in 
ringgit. Parcels not used for the production of the subject goods, and the parcel acquired from a 
private vendor, have been excluded from the numerator for the reasons already recorded, while 
the denominator remains the whole turnover of the respondent, the benefit not being tied to any 
particular product. 


58. Based upon the verified information available on record, the Authority determines that 
land was made available to the concerned exporters for less than adequate remuneration, 
resulting in a benefit to the recipients, that the programme is specific, and that the subsidy 
margins determined under this programme are as follows: 


Producer / Exporter Subsidy margin Range (%) 
Xinyi Energy Smart (Malaysia) Sdn. Bhd. | *** 0-l0 
Kibing Group (M) Sdn. Bhd. le 0-0 
Jinjing Technology Malaysia Sdn. Bhd. के कम 0-0 


Programme 2: Provision of Natural Gas for Less Than Adequate Remuneration 


59. Views of the Domestic Industry. The Government of Malaysia regulates domestic 
natural gas prices through Government-controlled entities and provides natural gas to industrial 
consumers, including float glass manufacturers, at prices below prevailing market prices. The 
regulated pricing framework enables eligible industrial users to procure natural gas at prices 
lower than international market benchmarks, thereby reducing production costs. The 
Government, through its pricing policy and regulatory framework, provides natural gas for less 
than adequate remuneration within the meaning of Article l4(d) of the SCM Agreement and 
Section 9 of the Act. The gas prices reported by the exporters should not be accepted in the 
absence of reliable and complete information from the Government of Malaysia, mere 
submission of invoice prices not establishing that such prices are representative of undistorted 
market prices, and appropriately adjusted Indonesian natural gas prices should be considered as 
an external benchmark, Indonesia being a geographically proximate and economically 
comparable regional benchmark. 


60. Submissions by the Government and other interested parties. The Government of 
Malaysia and the responding producers/exporters submitted that domestic natural gas prices are 
determined under the applicable statutory and regulatory framework and are not established for 
the benefit of any particular enterprise or industry. The responding producers/exporters 
purchased natural gas at the applicable regulated industrial tariff and did not receive any 


[भाग lars ] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 89 


preferential pricing or additional concession unavailable to other similarly situated industrial 
consumers. Copies of gas purchase agreements, invoices and payment records were furnished. 
The respondents disputed the benchmark proposed by the domestic industry and submitted that 
international LNG prices or export prices are not appropriate benchmarks for determining 
adequacy of remuneration under Article 4(d). 


6l. | Examination by the Authority — financial contribution. Domestic piped natural gas is 
supplied under a regulated framework in which a wholly State-owned corporation is the sole 
aggregator of domestic supply and in which the price payable is fixed under a mechanism 
administered by or on behalf of the Government. The supply of natural gas on those terms 
constitutes the provision of goods by the Government or by a public body within Article 
.l(a)()(@i) of the SCM Agreement and Section 9 of the Act. The Government of Malaysia has 
not disputed the regulated character of the framework. 


62. Examination by the Authority — specificity. The Government of Malaysia has 
submitted that domestic natural gas prices are determined under the applicable statutory and 
regulatory framework and are not established for the benefit of any particular enterprise or 
industry. The question is not whether the framework names the float glass industry. It is whether 
the regulated price is available to enterprises generally, or only to some. The Authority has 
examined the framework on that question and finds the availability limited in two distinct ways. 


63. First, limited by connection and by category. The regulated price is not available to 
any enterprise that wishes to buy gas. It is available only to an enterprise connected to the piped 
distribution network, and only in the area to which the framework extends; and within that 
network it is available only to an enterprise falling within one of the consumer categories 
designated under the applicable instruments, on terms fixed by or on behalf of the Government. 
An enterprise outside the network, or outside a designated category, cannot obtain gas at that 
price at all. A measure so confined is limited to certain enterprises within the meaning of Article 
2.l(a) of the SCM Agreement read with Annexure II to the Rules, and is in the alternative 
limited to enterprises located within a designated geographical region within the meaning of 
Article 2.2. 


64. Secondly, limited in fact by the nature of the benefit. Even upon the assumption that 
the regulated price is nominally open to a wide class, the advantage it confers is not evenly 
spread. The benefit of a price concession on gas accrues in proportion to the gas consumed. The 
manufacture of float glass requires a furnace held continuously at temperature and is among the 
most gas-intensive of industrial activities, and the responding producers/exporters are consumers 
of gas in very substantial quantities. A concession of this character is therefore used by, and is 
predominantly used by, a limited number of enterprises within the meaning of Article 2.l(c), 
whatever the terms of the instrument may say. 


65. The Authority accordingly determines that the programme is specific. The Authority 
records that the first ground rests upon the terms of the framework and the second upon its effect 
in fact, and that either ground is sufficient. 


66. Examination by the Authority — benefit. The Authority has considered whether the 
domestic reference price determined under the Malaysian regulatory framework may serve as the 
benchmark, and has concluded that it may not. That reference price is not a market price. It is the 
administered price fixed under the very framework which is alleged to confer the subsidy, in a 


90 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.!] 


market in which a wholly State-owned corporation is the sole aggregator of domestic supply. 
Where the government is the predominant provider and itself fixes the price, the in-country price 
is determined by the government’s own role in the market and cannot serve as a measure of the 
adequacy of the remuneration which the government receives; to adopt it would be to test the 
measure against itself. 


67. Having concluded that no usable in-country benchmark exists, the Authority has adopted 
an out-of-country benchmark, being the price at which natural gas was available on 
commercial terms in Indonesia outside the administered framework, that being the nearest 
regional market for which prices outside an administered framework are available in the period 
of investigation. The adoption of a regional benchmark of this description was proposed by the 
domestic industry, and Indonesia is geographically proximate and economically comparable to 
the subject country. 


68. The benchmark series applied follows the price which was in fact available in each 
month, the Authority having taken in each month the non-administered price at which gas could 
then be obtained, and not a single annual average. Specifically, for April 2024, the benchmark 
price taken was US$ .89 per MMBtu, corresponding to the non-administered pipeline price for 
the lowest published consumer category. For the period from May 2024 to December 2024, the 
benchmark price was US$ 3.00 per MMBtu based on the regasified liquefied natural gas price. 
Finally, for the period from January 2025 to March 2025, the benchmark price was US$ 6.77 
per MMBtu, reflecting the regasified liquefied natural gas price for that quarter. 


69. Regasified LNG pricing was introduced by the supplier only in May 2024, and for April 
2024 the pipeline price has accordingly been taken. That price is published by consumer 
category; the category applicable to each respondent is not on the record, and the Authority has 
taken the lowest published category, being the level least adverse to the respondents. The price 
of US$ 9.6 per MMBtu appearing on the record has not been adopted, having been superseded 
on 0.0.2023, six months before the period of investigation began. 


70. The Authority has examined the applicable regulatory framework, the gas supply 
agreements, the invoices and payment records, the competing submissions on the appropriate 
benchmark, and the benefit calculations placed on record. 


7l. Computation of the benefit. For each of the twelve months of the period of 
investigation the Authority has taken the quantity of natural gas purchased by the respondent in 
MMBtu and the price in fact paid by it per MMBtu, as appearing from its own invoices, and has 
multiplied the quantity by the amount, if any, by which the benchmark for that month exceeds 
the price paid. Where the benchmark for a month did not exceed the price paid, no benefit has 
been taken for that month. The numerator is the sum of the twelve monthly amounts so 
computed, converted into ringgit at the rate used by the respondent in its own response. The 
denominator is the total turnover of the respondent in the period of investigation in ringgit, the 
benefit not being tied to any particular product. The benefit is recurring and the whole of it has 
been taken into the period of investigation. Quantities invoiced in other units have been 
converted to MMBtu at the conversion factor used by the respondent itself. 


72. Based upon the verified information available on record, the Authority determines that 
natural gas was provided for less than adequate remuneration, that the programme constitutes a 


[भाग [खण्ड ] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 9] 


financial contribution which confers a countervailable benefit, and that the subsidy margins 
determined under this programme are as follows: 


Producer / Exporter Subsidy margin | Range 
(%) 
Xinyi Energy Smart (Malaysia) Sdn. Bhd. | *** 0-0 
Kibing Group (M) Sdn. Bhd. 52028 0-0 
Jinjing Technology Malaysia Sdn. Bhd. कक ले: 0-0 


Programme 3: Investment Tax Allowance and Reinvestment Allowance 


73. Views of the Domestic Industry. The Government of Malaysia grants Investment Tax 
Allowance to qualifying manufacturing enterprises undertaking approved investments, 
permitting deduction of a prescribed percentage of qualifying capital expenditure against 
statutory income and thereby reducing the income tax liability of eligible enterprises. The 
programme constitutes government revenue foregone and confers a measurable financial benefit. 


74. Submissions by the Government and other interested parties. The Government of 
Malaysia submitted the relevant provisions governing the Investment Tax Allowance under the 
Promotion of Investments Act, 986, and stated that none of the responding producers/exporters 
received Investment Tax Allowance benefits in relation to the subject goods during the period of 
investigation. Kibing Group (M) submitted that it had claimed benefit under this scheme. Xinyi 
Energy Smart (Malaysia) submitted that it had claimed an ITA-equivalent income-tax exemption 
and a Reinvestment Allowance during 2024 and furnished the relevant tax and approval 
documents. Jinjing Technology Malaysia submitted that it did not benefit from Investment Tax 
Allowance or investment-linked deductions during the period of investigation. 


73: Examination by the Authority. An allowance or exemption which reduces the tax 
otherwise chargeable constitutes government revenue otherwise due which is foregone within 
Article |.l(a)(l)Gi) of the SCM Agreement and Section 9 of the Act. The Investment Tax 
Allowance is not generally available: it is granted under the Promotion of Investments Act only 
in respect of approved promoted activities and promoted products, upon application to and 
approval by the Malaysian Investment Development Authority. A benefit confined to activities 
and products designated by the Government, and granted upon approval, is specific within the 
meaning of Article 2.l(a) and Article 2.l(c) of the SCM Agreement. The Reinvestment 
Allowance is confined to companies engaged in manufacturing which reinvest in qualifying 
projects and is accordingly limited to a group of industries within Article 2.l(a). The Authority 
notes that the submission of the Government of Malaysia that no benefit was received under 
these programmes is not correct, two of the participating exporters having accepted receipt of 
benefit thereunder, and one of them having disclosed a programme which the Government did 
not disclose at all. The Authority further notes that Jinjing Technology Malaysia held an income- 
tax exemption under the Northern Corridor Economic Region, which is examined as Programme 
fe 


92 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.!] 


76. The benefit conferred is the amount of tax which would have been payable but for the 
allowance or exemption. In the case of Xinyi Energy Smart (Malaysia), the Authority has 
adopted the tax reductions reported by the respondent and supported by its tax returns and 
approval documents, those being the verified measure of the revenue actually foregone. 


ae Computation of the benefit. An income-tax allowance operates by reducing the income 
on which tax is charged. The benefit is accordingly the tax which the respondent would have 
paid but for the allowance. The Authority has examined, in the case of each respondent, the 
allowance claimed and admitted in the year of assessment falling within the period of 
investigation, as appearing from its tax returns, assessments and approval documents. In the case 
of Xinyi Energy Smart (Malaysia) the Authority has satisfied itself that the amount so appearing 
is the allowance absorbed multiplied by the rate of corporate income tax applicable to that 
respondent, and has taken that amount as the benefit. In the case of Kibing Group (M) the 
Authority finds that the amount so appearing is the allowance itself and not the tax foregone 
thereon; this finding was expressly recorded in the Disclosure Statement. Having considered the 
post-disclosure comments of that respondent, the Authority has recomputed the benefit as the 
allowance absorbed multiplied by the rate of corporate income tax of 24 per cent applicable to 
that respondent, and has taken that amount as the benefit. The numerator in each case is the 
amount so determined. The denominator is the total turnover of the respondent in the period of 
investigation, the allowance not being tied to any particular product. The benefit is recurring, 
being conferred and consumed in the year of assessment in which the allowance is claimed, and 
the whole of it has been taken into the period of investigation; no part of it has been allocated 
over any longer period. Where an allowance was carried forward and not set off in that year, no 
benefit has been taken, there being in that event no tax foregone in the period of investigation. 


78. Based upon the verified information available on record, the Authority determines that 
these programmes constitute a financial contribution which is specific and which confers a 
countervailable benefit, and that the subsidy margins determined thereunder are as follows: 


Producer / Exporter Programme Subsidy Range (%) 
margin 
Kibing Group (M) Sdn. Bhd. Investment Tax Allowance | *** 0-0 
Xinyi Energy Smart (Malaysia) | Investment Tax Allowance | *** 0-0
