Programme 4: Industrial Building Allowance and Accelerated Capital Allowance 


79. Views of the Domestic Industry. The Government of Malaysia grants Industrial 
Building Allowance under Schedule 3 of the Income Tax Act 967 in respect of qualifying 
industrial buildings, and Accelerated Capital Allowance permitting accelerated depreciation of 
qualifying capital assets. Both reduce taxable income and constitute government revenue 
foregone. 


80. Submissions by the Government and other interested parties. The Government of 
Malaysia submitted the applicable statutory provisions and stated that none of the responding 
producers/exporters received benefits under either programme in relation to the subject goods 
during the period of investigation. Kibing Group (M) submitted that it had received benefit under 


[भाग [खण्ड ] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 93 


the Industrial Building Allowance. Xinyi Energy Smart (Malaysia) and Jinjing Technology 
Malaysia submitted that they had claimed benefit under the Accelerated Capital Allowance. 


8l. | Examination by the Authority. Both allowances reduce the tax otherwise chargeable 
and constitute revenue foregone within Article |.l(a)(l)(@i) of the SCM Agreement. As regards 
specificity, the Authority notes that neither allowance is available to every taxpayer upon 
incurring expenditure of any description. The Industrial Building Allowance is available only 
in respect of expenditure upon buildings of a description qualifying under the Schedule, that is to 
say buildings used for purposes of manufacture and like activities, and is therefore confined to 
enterprises engaged in those activities. The Accelerated Capital Allowance is not part of the 
general capital allowance regime but is conferred by rules made from time to time in respect of 
designated categories of assets and designated activities, and is available only to enterprises 
which acquire assets of those categories or carry on those activities. Each allowance is 
accordingly limited to certain enterprises within the meaning of Article 2.l(a) of the SCM 
Agreement read with Annexure II to the Rules. The Authority determines that both 
programmes are specific. The Authority has examined the questionnaire responses, tax records 
and verification findings placed on record. 


82. Computation of the benefit. Each of these allowances operates by permitting a 
deduction in respect of qualifying capital expenditure at a rate higher, or at a time earlier, than 
the depreciation otherwise allowable. The benefit is the tax deferred or foregone by reason of the 
accelerated or additional deduction, and the Authority has examined the allowance claimed and 
admitted by each respondent against its tax returns and assessments. In the case of Jinjing 
Technology Malaysia the Authority has satisfied itself that the amount so appearing is the 
allowance multiplied by the rate of corporate income tax applicable to that respondent, and has 
taken that amount as the benefit. In the case of Kibing Group (M) the Authority finds that the 
amount so appearing is the allowance itself; the benefit has accordingly been recomputed as the 
allowance multiplied by the rate of corporate income tax of 24 per cent applicable to that 
respondent, for the reasons stated in relation to the preceding programme. The numerator is the 
amount so determined and the denominator is the total turnover of the respondent in the period of 
investigation. The benefit has been treated as arising in, and confined to, the year of assessment 
in which the allowance was claimed, the deduction being taken in that year; it has accordingly 
not been allocated over the average useful life of the asset to which the expenditure relates, and 
only the amount attributable to the period of investigation has been countervailed. 


83. Based upon the verified information available on record, the Authority determines that 
these programmes confer a countervailable benefit, and that the subsidy margins determined 
thereunder are as follows: 


Producer / Exporter Programme Subsidy Range (%) 
margin 

Kibing Group (M) Sdn. Bhd. Industrial Building | *** 0-0 
Allowance 

Kibing Group (M) Sdn. Bhd. Accelerated Capital | *** 0-0

Programme 5: Sales Tax Exemption on Imported Raw Materials and Equipment 


84. Views of the Domestic Industry. The Government of Malaysia exempts eligible 
manufacturers from payment of sales tax on specified goods, raw materials, components and 
packaging materials. Such exemptions constitute government revenue foregone, reduce the 
production costs of eligible enterprises and confer a measurable financial benefit. 


85. Submissions by the Government and other interested parties. The Government of 
Malaysia submitted that the programme is governed by the Sales Tax (Persons Exempted from 
Payment of Tax) Order 208. Kibing Group (M) submitted that it availed the Schedule C sales 
tax exemption as a registered manufacturer, the exemption applying to raw materials, 
components and packaging used in manufacturing activities. Jinjing Technology Malaysia and 
Xinyi Energy Smart (Malaysia) submitted that they operate under the Licensed Manufacturing 
Warehouse framework and, in respect of sales tax, relied upon a determination of the U.S. 
Department of Commerce to the effect that the benefit of a sales tax exemption accrues to the 
purchaser rather than to the seller. 


86. Examination by the Authority. Exemption from a tax otherwise payable constitutes 
revenue foregone within Article |.l(a)()(ii) of the SCM Agreement. 


87. As regards the reliance placed upon the determination of the U.S. Department of 
Commerce, that determination is on record and has been examined. It contains two distinct 
holdings: first, that in respect of the exemption applicable to the finished product the benefit 
accrues to the purchaser and not to the seller, the producer’s role being limited to remitting a tax 
borne by the buyer; and secondly, that in respect of the exemption applicable to input materials 
the programme was not specific on the record then before that authority. The Authority is not 
bound by the determination of the investigating authority of another Member and considers it 
distinguishable in the present case for the following reasons: 


i. The first holding has no application to the programme in issue. It concerns tax upon the 
producer’s output sales. The exemption examined here is an exemption upon the 
respondent’s inputs and equipment, in respect of which the respondent is itself the 
purchaser and therefore, upon the reasoning of that determination, the party upon which 
the benefit falls. 


ii. That determination was made under the sales tax legislation then in force in Malaysia. The 
regime presently in issue was introduced with effect from 0.09.208 under the Sales Tax 
Act 20I8, and the instrument in issue is Schedule C of the Order made thereunder, which 
operates by application to and approval by the Royal Malaysian Customs Department of 
a registered manufacturer in respect of an approved list of goods. The respondent has 
itself described the exemption as availed “as a registered manufacturer”. A finding of 
non-specificity made upon the earlier legislation does not determine the character of the 
instrument now in issue. 


iii. That determination concerned a different product, a different period of investigation and 
different respondents, and the finding of non-specificity rested expressly upon the 
information which that authority was able to collect and verify upon the record of that 
case. 


[भाग [खण्ड ] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 95 


88. As regards specificity, the Authority determines, for the reasons recorded above in 
relation to the sales tax exemption on raw materials, that the exemption is not available as of 
right to all persons but is granted upon application, registration and approval of a specified 
manufacturer in respect of an approved list of goods, and is accordingly specific within the 
meaning of Article 2.l(a) and Article 2.l(c) of the SCM Agreement. 


89. | Computation of the benefit. The exemption operates upon goods brought in during the 
period of investigation, and the benefit is the sales tax which would have been payable upon 
those goods but for the exemption. The Authority has taken the value of the goods covered by 
the exemption certificates and entered during the period of investigation, as appearing from the 
respondent’s own customs entries and purchase records, and has applied to that value the rate of 
sales tax which would otherwise have been chargeable. The numerator is the amount so 
computed. The denominator is the total turnover of the respondent in the period of investigation. 
In the case of raw materials, consumables and packing materials the benefit is recurring, the 
goods being consumed in the year, and the whole of the amount has been taken into the period of 
investigation. In the case of equipment, the goods being capital assets employed in production 
over a number of years, the benefit is non-recurring and, after considering the post-disclosure 
comments of the respondent, has been allocated over the average useful life of fifteen years, only 
one-fifteenth of it having been taken into the period of investigation. The Authority has 
examined the exemption certificates, customs entries and purchase records relating to both 
components and has satisfied itself as to the amounts so determined. The rate determined upon 
equipment reflects that allocation. 


90. Based upon the verified information available on record, the Authority determines that 
the programme constitutes a financial contribution which is specific and which confers a 
countervailable benefit, and that the subsidy margins determined thereunder are as follows: 


Producer / Exporter Component Subsidy margin | Range (%) 

Kibing Group (M) Sdn. | Raw materials hehe 0—-0 

Bhd. 

Kibing Group (M) Sdn. | Equipment ore 0-0 

Bhd. 

Programme 6: Exemption from Import Duty on Raw Materials, Machinery and Spare Parts 
9l. Views of the Domestic Industry. The Government of Malaysia grants exemption from 


import duty on imported raw materials, components, machinery, spares and consumables used in 
manufacturing activities. Such exemptions reduce the cost of production and constitute 
government revenue foregone within Article |.l(a)()@i) of the SCM Agreement, and are 
specific to enterprises satisfying the prescribed eligibility conditions. 


92. Submissions by the Government and other interested parties. The Government of 
Malaysia submitted that exemptions from import duty on raw materials and components are 
governed by the Customs Act 967 and related customs notifications. All three participating 
Malaysian producers/exporters submitted that they availed exemption from import duty on raw 
materials, spare parts, accessories and machinery — two of them under the Licensed 
Manufacturing Warehouse scheme established under Section 65A of the Customs Act 967, and 


96 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.]] 


the third under the applicable customs framework. It was further submitted that exemption from 
import GST is merely a creditable indirect tax and therefore does not constitute a subsidy, and, in 
relation to a separate allegation, that exemptions relating to import duty and sales tax on spares, 
machinery, equipment and raw materials arise automatically under the preferential tariff 
treatment available under the India-ASEAN Free Trade Agreement upon submission of the 
prescribed Certificate of Origin, and accordingly do not involve a financial contribution by the 
Government of Malaysia. 


93. | Examination by the Authority. The Authority accepts the contention in relation to 
import GST: a creditable indirect tax which the taxpayer would in any event recover confers no 
benefit, and no amount has been countervailed on that account. The Authority likewise accepts 
the contention in relation to preferential tariff treatment under a free trade agreement: such 
treatment is not a subsidy and no amount has been countervailed on that account. 


94. Exemption from import duty otherwise payable constitutes revenue foregone within 
Article l.l(a)(l)(@i) of the SCM Agreement. A Licensed Manufacturing Warehouse licence is 
granted upon application to and approval by the Royal Malaysian Customs Department, and the 
exemption is available only to licence holders in respect of an approved list of goods. The 
programme is accordingly specific within the meaning of Article 2.l(a) and Article 2.l(c) of 
the SCM Agreement. The Authority notes that in the case of one respondent the import duty 
exempted on raw materials is nil, the applicable tariff upon its raw material imports being zero, 
the benefit arising only upon machinery and spare parts. 


95. Computation of the benefit. The benefit is the import duty which would have been 
payable upon the goods entered under the exemption but for the exemption. The Authority has 
taken the assessable value of the goods entered during the period of investigation under each 
exemption, as appearing from the respondent’s own customs entries, and has applied to that 
value the rate of import duty which would otherwise have been chargeable under the Malaysian 
customs tariff for the heading concerned. The numerator is the amount so computed; the 
denominator is the total turnover of the respondent in the period of investigation. The Authority 
has examined the customs entries of each respondent and the amounts of duty foregone 
appearing from them. In the case of Kibing Group (M) the duty foregone upon raw materials has 
been taken in full into the period of investigation. In the case of Xinyi Energy Smart (Malaysia) 
and of Jinjing Technology Malaysia the Authority finds that the duty foregone upon machinery 
and upon spare parts has been reported as a single amount and allocated over a period of twelve 
years, the basis of that period not being stated in either annexure. In their post-disclosure 
comments, the said respondents have submitted that the allocation period is supported by their 
questionnaire responses and accounting records and should be retained. Having examined the 
same, the Authority has treated the benefit, which relates to capital assets employed in 
production over a number of years, as non-recurring, and has allocated it over the average useful 
life of fifteen years adopted in the methodology, consistently with the treatment of the exemption 
on equipment in the case of Kibing Group (M) Sdn. Bhd. The rates determined upon machinery 
and spare parts are accordingly very small and are shown to four decimal places; the Authority 
records that such a rate is not a determination that no benefit was conferred, but that the amount 
attributable to the period of investigation is negligible.

the programme constitutes a financial contribution which is specific and which confers a 
countervailable benefit, and that the subsidy margins determined thereunder are as follows:

Programme 7: Income-Tax Exemption under the Northern Corridor Economic Region 


97. Origin and legal basis. This programme was not disclosed by the Government of 
Malaysia. It was raised by the domestic industry. The respondent referred to it in its 
questionnaire response, stating that it had received no actual benefit under it, and furnished its 
tax records in support. The programme is established from those records and from the 
respondent’s own audited financial statements for the year 2024 and its income-tax return for the 
year of assessment 2024. The exemption is granted under P.U.(A) 2/2006, the Income Tax 
(Exemption) (No. 2) Order 2006, made under section 27(3)(b) of the Income Tax Act 967, 
through the Northern Corridor Implementation Authority, and exempts l00 per cent of the 
statutory income from approved activities for the years of assessment 2022 to 2026, extendable 
to 203l. The respondent’s own records establish that the exemption was in force throughout the 
period of investigation. 


98. Examination by the Authority. An exemption from tax otherwise payable constitutes 
government revenue otherwise due which is foregone, within Article |.l(a)()(ii) of the SCM 
Agreement. The exemption is available only to enterprises located within a designated 
geographical region and approved by the administering authority, and is accordingly specific 
within the meaning of Article 2.2 and, in the alternative, Article 2.l(a) of the SCM Agreement. 


99. Computation of the benefit. The benefit is the tax which would have been payable but 
for the exemption, in the year of assessment falling within the period of investigation. The 
respondent’s tax records for that year show that no tax was foregone under the programme, the 
tax payable being the same with or without the exemption. The Authority accordingly determines 
that no countervailable benefit arose under this programme in the period of investigation, which 
is consistent with the explanation given by the respondent in its questionnaire response. The 
programme may be examined in any future proceeding. The subsidy margin under this 
programme is accordingly as follows: 


Producer / Exporter Subsidy margin Range (%) 


Jinjing Technology Malaysia Sdn. Bhd. Nil 0-0

H.2 Programmes determined not to be countervailable 
Policy Lending from Chinese Banks / BRI Capacity Cooperation Projects 


00. The Authority sought clarification regarding alleged policy lending from Chinese State- 
owned banks under BRI(Belt and Road Initiative) related programmes, including whether the 
participating exporters had received any financing directly or indirectly. Jinjing Technology 
Malaysia and Xinyi Energy Smart (Malaysia) each submitted that they had not received any 
direct loan under the alleged programme and that there was no BRI-linked investment, while 
providing without prejudice a calculation of policy lending. Kibing Group (M) submitted that it 
had obtained a loan from its Chinese parent company and that the parent company had not 
obtained any loan under the BRI Industrial Policy, directly or indirectly. 


0!. Examination by the Authority. The Authority has examined the submissions and notes 
that the loans in question were availed by the Chinese entities in China and not by the 
participating exporters from financial institutions in the subject countries. The subsequent 
transfer of funds by the Chinese parent or related entities to their Malaysian affiliates does not, 
by itself, establish the provision of a financial contribution by the Government of Malaysia or a 
benefit to the Malaysian producers/exporters under the alleged programme. Accordingly, in the 
absence of evidence of a financial contribution by the Government of a subject country to the 
participating exporters, the said loans cannot be treated as a countervailable subsidy in the 
present investigation. 


Provision of Electricity for Less Than Adequate Remuneration 


02. The Authority sought details of the applicable electricity tariff, electricity consumption 
and the relevant tariff classification. Jinjing Technology Malaysia submitted that it was covered 
under tariff category N6 and furnished electricity bills and consumption information. Kibing 
Group (M) submitted that it was covered under Tariff E2, being the Medium Voltage Peak/Off- 
Peak Industrial Tariff, and furnished electricity bills. Xinyi Energy Smart (Malaysia) reported 
tariff categories E2 and E3 and furnished electricity bills and consumption information. Each 
submitted that the tariff applied uniformly to industrial consumers falling within the relevant 
category and was not specific to the glass industry. 


03. Examination by the Authority. The Authority has examined the tariff categories 
reported, the electricity bills and consumption information furnished on record, and the published 
tariff schedules of the Malaysian electricity supply industry placed on record. Upon that 
examination the Authority notes that: 


i. the unit rates recorded in the respondents’ billing correspond to the published tariff 
schedule for the tariff category applicable to each respondent; 


il. the published tariffs apply to all industrial consumers meeting the voltage and maximum 
demand criteria of the relevant category, those being objective technical criteria; 


ili. the published schedules provide for separate special industrial tariff categories available 
only to qualified consumers at materially lower rates, and none of the respondents was 
supplied under any such category; and 


[भाग [खण्ड ] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 99 


iv. the billing discloses no element borne by the Government, the scheduled rates having 
been applied and the applicable fuel cost pass-through and renewable energy fund 
charges having been levied upon the consumer in addition thereto, and the rates payable 
having risen during the period of investigation. 


04. Based upon the verified information available on record, the Authority determines that 
the provision of electricity in Malaysia is not specific within the meaning of Article 2 of the 
SCM Agreement and does not confer a countervailable benefit upon the participating 
producers/exporters, and no amount has been countervailed on that account.

06. In respect of the programmes at serial numbers 8 and 22, the Authority additionally 
notes that the responding producers/exporters do not satisfy the statutory definition of a small or

the Government of Malaysia that the drawback programme is administered by the Royal 
Malaysian Customs Department through case-by-case applications for refund of import duties 
paid on imported goods that are subsequently re-exported, and that it conforms to Annexes I, II 
and III of the SCM Agreement. The Authority records that a drawback scheme confined to the 
remission of duty actually levied upon inputs consumed in the production of exported products is 
not a subsidy, and that only excess remission is countervailable. No respondent having availed 
the programme, it is unnecessary to determine whether any excess remission arises and the 
Authority does not do so. 


H.4 Programme availed or alleged, no countervailable benefit established 


08. Buyer Credit Guarantee. The domestic industry alleged that the Government of 
Malaysia provides guarantees supporting credit extended to buyers of Malaysian exports. The 
Government of Malaysia has identified the programme as the Bankers Trade Credit Takaful 
administered by the Export-Import Bank of Malaysia Berhad, which protects financial 
institutions against the risk of non-payment arising from default by overseas buyers, and has 
stated that no responding producer or exporter received any benefit under it during the period of 
investigation. Xinyi Energy Smart (Malaysia) and Kibing Group (M) each stated that neither 
they nor their overseas buyers received any financing or guarantee under the programme. The 
protection under the programme is afforded to the financial institution and not to the producer or 
exporter, so that a benefit to a producer or exporter does not follow from the existence of the 
programme but must be established; and an export credit guarantee or insurance programme is 
countervailable where the premium rates are inadequate to cover the long-term operating costs 
and losses of the programme (item (j) of Annexure III to the Rules). No material bearing upon 
the premium rates, or upon the long-term operating costs and losses of the programme, has been 
placed upon the record by any party, nor has the domestic industry proposed any comparator. In 
the absence of such material the Authority determines that a countervailable benefit under this 
programme has not been established upon the record of this investigation, and no amount has 
accordingly been countervailed.

ASSESSMENT OF INJURY AND CAUSAL LINK 
J. METHODOLOGY FOR INJURY ASSESSMENT 


i2. Rule I3 of the Rules deals with the principles governing the determination of injury and 
provides as follows: 


+]३ Determination of injury.— (J) In the case of imports from specified countries, the 
designated authority shall give a further finding that the import of such article into 
India causes or threatens material injury to any industry established in India, or 
materially retards the establishment of an industry in India. (2) Except when a finding 
of injury is made under sub-rule (3), the designated authority shall determine the 
injury, threat of injury, material retardation to the establishment of an industry and the 
causal link between the subsidized import and the injury, taking into account inter alia, 
the principle laid down in Annexure I to the rule. (3) The designated authority may, in 
exceptional cases, give a finding as to the existence of injury even where a substantial 
portion of the domestic industry is not injured if — (i) there is a concentration of 
subsidized imports into an isolated market, and ता) the subsidized imports are causing 
injury to the producers of almost all of the production within such market.” 


l3. The Authority records that the present determination proceeds upon the basis of present 
material injury. Neither threat of material injury nor material retardation of the establishment of 
an industry is claimed or determined. 


गैं.। De minimis, negligibility of import volumes and cumulation 


4. Rule 6(4)(c) and Rule 6(4)(d) of the Rules require the Authority to terminate an 
investigation immediately, by issue of a public notice, if it determines that the amount of subsidy 
is less than one per cent ad valorem, or in the case of a product originating from a developing 
country the amount of subsidy is less than two per cent; or that the volume of the subsidised 
imports, actual or potential, or the injury where applicable, is negligible, or in the case of a 
product originating in a developing country the volume of the subsidised imports represents less 
than four per cent of the total imports of the like product into India, unless imports from 
developing countries whose individual shares of total imports represent less than four per cent 
collectively account for more than nine per cent of the total imports of the like product into India. 
The said provisions give effect to Article 27.l0(a) and Article 27.l0(b) of the SCM Agreement. 
The obligation is couched in mandatory terms, and where the threshold is attracted no discretion 
survives to continue the investigation in respect of the country concerned. 


5. Status of the subject countries as developing countries. Neither Section 9 of the Act 
nor the Rules define the expression “developing country”, and no list of developing countries has 


04 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.!] 


been notified for the purposes of Section 9 or of the Rules. Indonesia and Malaysia are classified 
as developing economies in the classification of economies maintained by UNCTAD upon the 
basis of the Standard Country or Area Codes for Statistical Use (M49) of the United Nations 
Statistics Division, and stand notified as developing countries at Serial No. 53 and Serial No. 69 
respectively of Notification No. l9/20l6-Customs (N.T.) dated 05.02.20l6  [F. 
No. 2000/22/20I5-OSD(UICD)], issued in supersession of Notification No. l03/98-Customs 
dated 4.2.998. Both countries are developing country Members of the World Trade 
Organization for the purposes of Article 27 of the SCM Agreement. The Authority notes that 
Notification No. 9/206-Customs (N.T.) has been issued under clause (a) of sub-section (6) of 
Section 8B of the Act, that is to say for the purposes of safeguard measures, and that there is no 
corresponding notification under Section 9; the notification is nonetheless the only enumeration 
of developing countries made by the Central Government under the Act, and is corroborative of a 
status which rests primarily upon the position of the two countries as developing country 
Members under the SCM Agreement. The Authority determines that Indonesia and Malaysia 
are each a developing country for the purposes of Rule 6(l) of the Rules. 


l6. The Authority further notes that the graduation of a Member from paragraph (b) of 
Annex VII to the SCM Agreement is of no consequence to the present question. Annex VII(b) 
governs the exemption from the prohibition upon export subsidies and the differentiated de 
minimis level under Article 27.; the four per cent volume threshold under Article 27.l0(b), as 
incorporated in Rule l6(.)(d), applies to all developing country Members without distinction, 
and the Rules prescribe only two de minimis subsidy levels, of one per cent generally and two 
per cent for a developing country. 


l7. De minimis — Rule 6()(c). The subsidy margins determined in relevant Section 
exceed two per cent ad valorem in the case of each participating producer/exporter of Malaysia 
and in the case of the residual margin determined for that country. No margin determined is de 
minimis, whether upon the general standard of one per cent or upon the standard of two per cent 
applicable to a product originating from a developing country. Rule 6()(c) is accordingly not 
attracted in respect of Malaysia. No subsidy margin having been determined in respect of 
Indonesia, Rule 6(l)(c) does not fall for application to that country; the investigation in respect 
of Indonesia is terminated under Rule !6(/)(d) upon the volume of imports. 


8. Volume of subsidised imports — Rule 6(l)(d). The volume of imports of the like 
article into India from each subject country and from all other sources, expressed as a share of 
the total imports of the like article into India from all sources, is as follows:

l9. Imports from Malaysia constituted 88.95% of the total imports of the like article into 
India during the period of investigation and were above the four per cent threshold in each year 
of the injury period. The threshold prescribed under Rule 6()(d) is not attracted in respect 
of Malaysia. 


[भाग are ] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 05 


20. Imports from Indonesia were 2,858 MT during the period of investigation, being 
3.40% of the total imports of the like article into India of 3,78,226 MT, which is below the 
threshold of four per cent prescribed under Rule 6(4)(d) of the Rules. The share of 
Indonesia was below four per cent in each year of the injury period. The share has been 
computed upon the volume of the total imports of the like article into India from all sources, and 
not upon the imports from the subject countries alone or upon demand in India, and upon volume 
and not upon value. The volumes and shares relied upon in this head are drawn from the 
transaction-wise import data of the Directorate General of Systems and Data Management, 
restricted to the product under consideration. The post-disclosure submission of the domestic 
industry questioning the share of imports from Bangladesh has been examined in Section Q 
below, and the country-wise composition set out below is confirmed. 


2l. The exemption under Rule |6(/)(d) is not, however, established by the individual share 
alone. Two further matters fall for determination before any conclusion is recorded, namely the 
collective proviso and the potential volume of the subsidised imports. The first is determined 
below upon the country-wise import data on record; the second is examined thereafter: 


i. The collective proviso. The exemption stands displaced where imports from developing 
countries, each of which individually accounts for less than four per cent of the total 
imports of the like article into India, collectively account for more than nine per cent of 
such total imports. Only developing countries individually below four per cent are 
aggregated for this purpose; countries not entitled to developing-country treatment, and 
developing countries individually at or above four per cent, are excluded from the 
aggregation, the proviso applying by its terms to “developing countries whose individual 
shares of total imports represent less than four per cent”. 


il. The country-wise composition of the total imports of the like article into India during the 
period of investigation, and the application of the proviso to each source, is as follows: 


Country of origin | Volume (MT) Share of total imports | Individually below four per

i. Imports from Bangladesh were 9,482 MT during the period of investigation, being 
5.5% of the total imports of the like article into India. Bangladesh is accordingly not a 


06 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.]] 


country “whose individual share of total imports represents less than four per cent”, and 
its volume is excluded from the aggregation under the proviso upon the same ground as 
that upon which the volume of Malaysia is excluded. The Authority records that this 
exclusion is determinative of the present question: were the volume of Bangladesh to be 
brought into the aggregation, the collective share would be .05% of the total imports 
and the proviso would be attracted. The exclusion follows, however, from the express 
words of the proviso, which admit of aggregation only of those developing countries 
whose individual shares are below four per cent, and the Authority can neither add to nor 
subtract from those words. 


ii. Upon the volumes of Malaysia and of Bangladesh standing excluded, the remaining 


ili. 


sources individually below four per cent, other than Indonesia, accounted for 9,445 MT 
during the period of investigation, being 2.497% of the total imports of the like article 
into India. Aggregating that volume in its entirety with the 3.400% of Indonesia, the 
collective share is 5.897% of the total imports of the like article into India, which is 
below the nine per cent threshold prescribed under Rule 6(l)(d) of the Rules. The 
Authority has deliberately adopted for this purpose the assumption most adverse to the 
exemption, namely that every source individually below four per cent is a developing 
country entitled to aggregation, whatever its actual status may be. Upon the narrower and 
more accurate basis, Spain and Romania not being developing countries, the collective 
share is lower still, and the determination therefore does not turn upon the status of any 
individual third country. 


The proviso to Rule 6(I)(d) is accordingly not attracted. The Authority further notes 
that the conclusion is not sensitive to the completeness of the import compilation. 
Bangladesh would fall below the four per cent threshold, and so enter the aggregation, 
only if the total imports of the like article into India exceeded 4,87,050 MT, that is to say 
only if the compilation relied upon were understated by more than ,08,824 MT; and 
even upon that hypothesis the collective share of every source individually below four 
per cent, Bangladesh included, would be 8.58% and would remain below the nine per 
cent threshold. 


iv. Potential volume. Rule |6(l)(d) refers to the volume of the subsidised imports “actual or 


Ie. 
investigation and in each year of the injury period. The collective proviso is not attracted. As 
regards potential volume, nothing on the record establishes, upon positive evidence, any quantity 
capable of being directed to India above the threshold: no party has alleged a figure, no capacity 
or capacity-utilisation data for the producers of the subject goods in Indonesia has been furnished 


potential”. Where the record discloses idle or expanding capacity in the subject country, 
an established export orientation and freely disposable quantities capable of being 
directed to India, a potential volume above the threshold may be established 
notwithstanding a low share during the period of investigation; such a determination must 
rest upon positive evidence and not upon conjecture. The Authority examined the 
capacity, capacity utilisation, export orientation and freely disposable quantities of the 
producers of the subject goods in Indonesia upon the material on record, and notes in this 
regard that two producers identified by the Government of Indonesia commenced 
commercial operation towards the close of the period of investigation. 


Determination. The individual share of Indonesia is below four per cent in the period of 


[भाग [---खण्ड |] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 07 


by the Government of Indonesia or by any other party, and a determination of potential volume 
cannot rest upon the bare fact that producers exist. The Authority accordingly terminates the 
investigation, in terms of Rule l6(/)(d) of the Rules, in respect of imports of the subject goods 
originating in or exported from Indonesia. The investigation stands continued in respect of 
Malaysia, and imports from Indonesia stand excluded from the assessment of the effects of the 
subsidised imports and from any recommendation. 


23. Cumulation. Article 5.3 of the SCM Agreement and paragraph (ii) of Annexure I to 
the Rules provide that where imports of a product from more than one country are being 
simultaneously subjected to anti-subsidy investigation, the Authority will cumulatively assess the 
effect of such imports in case it determines that: 


a. the amount of subsidisation established in relation to the imports from each country is 
more than one per cent ad valorem and the volume of imports from each country is not 
negligible; and 


b. a cumulative assessment of the effects of the imports is appropriate in the light of the 
conditions of competition between the imported products and the like domestic product. 


24. Cumulation does not arise. The condition precedent to a cumulative assessment is that 
imports from more than one country are simultaneously subjected to investigation. Upon the 
termination in respect of Indonesia, the imports of only one subject country, Malaysia, remain 
under investigation. There is accordingly nothing to cumulate, and the question whether a 
cumulative assessment would be appropriate in the light of the conditions of competition does 
not fall for determination. 


25. The assessment of injury in this Section has accordingly been carried out upon the 
imports from Malaysia alone. The volume, landed value and price parameters set out below are 
those of Malaysia, drawn from the transaction-wise import data restricted to the product under 
consideration. The Authority records, for completeness, that the exclusion of Indonesia does not 
materially alter any parameter: upon the cumulated imports of both countries the landed value 
moved from = 30,64 per MT in the base year to = 9,876 per MT in the period of investigation, 
against = 30,708 per MT and 2 9,842 per MT upon the imports of Malaysia alone, and the price 
undercutting in the period of investigation is in the range of 30-40% upon either basis. The range 
of undercutting is unchanged in every year of the injury period. 


K. VOLUME EFFECT OF THE SUBSIDIZED IMPORTS 
K. Submissions made on behalf of the opposing interested parties 


26. The submissions of the opposing interested parties with regard to injury and causal link 
are as follows: 


i. That the domestic industry’s own data show significant improvements in demand, sales, 
production, capacity, production of the product under consideration and employment 
during the injury period, with only profitability deteriorating, which is inconsistent with a 
finding of material injury. 


il. That imports from Indonesia remained insignificant during most of the injury period and 
constituted only a small share of total imports during the period of investigation. The

decline in profitability is attributable to the domestic industry’s capacity expansion, 
increased capital expenditure and other internal factors, rather than Indonesian imports. 
Inefficiencies and expansion-related costs should be excluded while determining the non- 
injurious price. 


That imports from Malaysia declined during the period of investigation despite rising 
demand, and that the domestic industry increased its sales and market share during the 
same period. The deterioration in profitability resulted from higher depreciation and 
interest costs arising from substantial capital investment, and not from subject imports. 


That declining capacity utilisation, rising inventories and deterioration in return on

rather than injury caused by imports. 


That no causal link exists between imports from Indonesia and the alleged injury, given 


the low volume of Indonesian imports and the failure to conduct a proper non-attribution 
analysis; and that in terms of Article 5.5 of the SCM Agreement and Annexure I to the 
Rules the Authority must distinguish the effects of subsidised imports from those of other 
known factors. 


K.2 Submissions made on behalf of the Domestic Industry 


27. 


The submissions of the domestic industry with regard to injury and causal link are as 


follows: 


i. That despite the presence of the domestic industry and other producers, the imports have

dominated the market, and imports from the subject countries constituted a significant 
share of the market during the period of investigation. 


That the subsidised imports are undercutting the prices of the domestic industry, the 
undercutting being significantly positive during the period of investigation, and that the 
subject imports have continuously caused strain on the prices of the domestic industry, 
being priced lower than its selling price throughout the injury period. 


That in the period of investigation the landed value of the subject goods was below both 
the cost of sales and the selling price of the domestic industry, demonstrating price 
pressure; that the subsidised imports have had a suppressing effect upon the prices of the 
domestic industry; and that due to the constant pressure of subsidised imports the 
domestic industry has been unable to dispose of its production sufficiently and its 
inventory has accumulated. 


That the interested parties have selectively relied upon positive economic indicators while 
ignoring the significant deterioration in profitability, cash profits, return on investment 
and price parameters. 


That the deterioration in performance is attributable to significant price undercutting and 


price suppression caused by subsidised imports, and that the increase in depreciation and 
interest costs arising from capacity expansion reflects commercial investment decisions 
undertaken to meet growing demand and cannot by itself explain or negate the injury 
suffered.

vi. That the existence of other factors, including expansion-related costs, does not sever the 
causal link where subsidised imports have materially suppressed domestic prices. 


K.3 Examination by the Authority 


28. In consideration of the various submissions made by the interested parties and the 
domestic industry, the Authority has examined the injury to the domestic industry on account of 
subsidised imports from Malaysia. The injury analysis made hereunder addresses the various 
submissions made by the interested parties. The contention that imports from Indonesia 
constituted only a small share of the total imports has been accepted and has been given effect 
under the head of negligibility of import volumes above, the investigation in respect of that 
country having been terminated; imports from Indonesia are accordingly treated hereafter as 
imports from a non-subject source. The submissions of the interested parties directed to 
Indonesia, and to the absence of a causal link between Indonesian imports and the injury 
claimed, do not fall for further consideration.

ii. The share of Malaysia in total imports increased from 60.56% in the base year to 88.95%

declined from 39.44% to .05%. 


ili. The share of Malaysia in Indian demand increased from a range of 5—l0% in the base

iv. Demand for the subject goods increased by 30% over the injury period; however, the 
market share of the domestic industry declined and the share of Malaysia in demand 
increased. 


3l. The Authority has considered the contention that imports from Malaysia declined 
during the period of investigation. It is correct that imports from Malaysia declined from 
3,6,898 MT in 2023-24 to 3,36,44] MT in the period of investigation, and that the domestic 
sales of the domestic industry increased over the same period with a recovery in its market share. 
The Authority determines, however, that the injury suffered during the period of 
investigation is predominantly a price effect and not a volume effect. Over the same period 
the landed value of the subject imports declined to an index of 65, price undercutting reached its 
highest level of the injury period, the selling price of the domestic industry declined to an index 
of 93 while its cost of sales rose to an index of 43, and the domestic industry moved from profit 
into loss. The domestic industry retained volume only by conceding price. Injury under 
Annexure I falls to be assessed upon volume and price effects together, and a finding of material 
injury does not require that import volumes rise in the period of investigation. 


L. PRICE EFFECT OF THE SUBSIDIZED IMPORTS 


32. In terms of Annexure I[(iii) to the Rules, with regard to the effect of the subsidised 
imports on prices, the Authority is required to consider whether there has been significant price 
undercutting by the subsidised imports as compared with the price of the like product in India, or 
whether the effect of such imports is otherwise to depress prices to a significant degree or to 
prevent price increases which otherwise would have occurred to a significant degree. 


L. Price undercutting 


33. Price undercutting has been determined by comparing the net sales realisation of the 
domestic industry with the landed price of the imports, as below:

rising to a range of 30-40% in the period of investigation. The Authority determines that during 
the period of investigation the subject imports were significantly undercutting the prices of the 
domestic industry. 


L.2 Price suppression and price depression 


35. In order to determine whether the subsidised imports are depressing the domestic prices, 
and whether the effect of such imports is to suppress prices to a significant degree or prevent 
price increases which otherwise would have occurred in the normal course, the changes in costs 


and prices over the injury period were compared as below:

36. Price depression. It is noted that the selling price of the domestic industry declined in 


absolute terms from an index of 2 in 2022-23 to an index of 93 in the period of investigation, 
ending the injury period 7% below the base year, while the landed value of the subject imports 
declined over the same period to an index of 65. The Authority determines that the subsidised 
imports have had a depressing effect upon the prices of the domestic industry. 


37. Price suppression. Over the same period the cost of sales of the domestic industry 
increased by 43%. The domestic industry was thereby prevented from raising its prices in 
tandem with its costs and was on the contrary compelled to reduce them. The Authority 
determines that the subsidised imports have prevented price increases which would 
otherwise have occurred, to a significant degree, and have thereby had a suppressing effect 
upon the prices of the domestic industry. 


38. It is further noted that during the period of investigation the landed value of the subject 
goods remained lower than both the cost of sales and the domestic selling price of the domestic 
industry.

on the state of the industry. The various injury parameters relating to the domestic industry are 
discussed below.

increased substantially during the injury investigation period, the growth in the price and 
financial parameters of the domestic industry was significantly negative in the period of 
investigation, which is reflective of the deterioration in its performance. 


M.7 Ability to raise capital investment 


46. The domestic industry has submitted that it has incurred steep losses and is facing 
negative returns, that its earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortisation have 
continuously deteriorated over the injury period and remained negative, and that there is 
consequently a negative impact upon its ability to raise capital investment. The Authority notes 
that the domestic industry moved from profit into loss during the period of investigation, 
that its cash profit and its return on capital employed turned negative, and that it 
undertook substantial capital investment over the injury period which it has been servicing 
out of a deteriorating income stream. The Authority determines that the ability of the 
domestic industry to raise capital investment has been adversely affected. 


M.8 Magnitude of the subsidy margin 


47. The margin of subsidy is an indicator of the extent to which the subsidised imports can 
cause injury to the domestic industry. The subsidy margins determined in relevant Section are 
positive, significant and above the de minimis level in respect of Malaysia, the sole subject 
country remaining under investigation. 


M.9 Factors affecting the prices of the domestic industry 


48. It is noted that the domestic industry has not been able to increase its prices to a 
remunerative level during the period of investigation. The imports have forced the domestic 
industry to sell the goods below cost, and the low-priced imports have also resulted in reduced 
market share and underutilized capacity. The subject imports have thus adversely affected the 
prices of the domestic industry. 


M.0 Conclusion on injury 


49. Having examined all the parameters set out above, the Authority concludes that the 
domestic industry has suffered material injury during the period of investigation in the form of 
significant price undercutting, price depression and price suppression, resulting in losses, cash 
losses, negative return on investment, accumulation of inventories and declining capacity 
utilisation, notwithstanding the growth in its volume parameters. Accordingly, the Authority 
determines that the domestic industry has suffered material injury on account of the subsidised 
imports from Malaysia. 


[भाग [खण्ड |] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण i5 


N. NON-ATTRIBUTION ANALYSIS 


50. Having examined the existence of injury and the volume and price effects of the 
subsidised imports, the Authority has examined whether injury to the domestic industry can be 
attributed to any factor other than the subsidised imports, as listed under the Rules and in 
accordance with Article 5.5 of the SCM Agreement, and has separated and distinguished the 
injurious effects of such other factors. 


N.l Capacity expansion, depreciation and interest cost 


5l. The opposing interested parties have contended that the deterioration in profitability is 
attributable to the domestic industry’s own capacity expansion and to the resulting depreciation 
and interest burden rather than to the subject imports. The domestic industry has answered that 
the expansion was a commercial response to growing demand and cannot by itself explain or 
negate the injury. 


52. Examination by the Authority. The Authority accepts that capacity expansion of the 
order undertaken by the domestic industry will have increased its depreciation and interest 
charges and hence its cost of sales, and that a part of the increase in the cost of sales over the 
injury period is attributable to that expansion and not to the subject imports. To that extent the 
contention is well founded and the effect has not been attributed to the subsidised imports. 


53. The contention nonetheless does not explain the injury suffered. Capacity expansion 
explains rising costs. It does not explain falling prices. Over the injury period the cost of sales 
of the domestic industry rose by 43% while its selling price fell by 7%. A producer facing rising 
costs in an undistorted market would raise its prices, or at the least hold them; the domestic 
industry reduced them, in a growing market, in the face of imports whose landed value declined 
to an index of 65 and which undercut its prices in the range of 30-40% in the period of 
investigation. The divergence between a 43% increase in cost and a 7% reduction in price is 
a price effect of the subsidised imports and is not explained by any internal factor 
advanced. 


54. The Authority further notes that the under-recovery of fixed costs arising from the 
decline in capacity utilisation from 95% to 86% is itself a consequence of the inability of the 
domestic industry to sell its production in the face of the subsidised imports, as evidenced by the 
increase in average inventories to 382% of the base year level in a market in which demand rose 
by 30%. That effect is properly attributed to the subject imports and not to the expansion which 
created the capacity. 


N.2 Volume and price of imports from third countries 


55. Upon the termination in respect of Indonesia, imports from that country fall to be 
examined under this head together with the imports of every other non-subject source. The 
volume and the landed value of imports from all sources other than Malaysia are as follows: 


Particulars UoM | 202-22 2022-23 2023-24 POI | 


Volume, all sources other than | MT 87,02] 56,507 94,992 4,785 
Malaysia 


Share of total imports % 39.44% 4.03% 20.79% .05% 


l6 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.!] 


Share of Indian demand % 5—0 0-5 0-5 0-5 


Landed value, sources other than | रे/शा' 20,254 2,l92 8,72 6,965 
Malaysia 


Landed value, Malaysia 2/MT_ | 30,708 29,939 22,323 9,842 


56. Volume. Imports from the non-subject sources fell from 87,02] MT in the base year to 
4,785 MT in the period of investigation, and their share of Indian demand from a range of 5— 
0% to a range of 0-5%. The Authority does not describe the movement as a continuous decline: 
the volume rose to 94,992 MT in 2023-24, being 20.79% of total imports and above the base- 
year volume, before falling in the period of investigation. Taken across the injury period as a 
whole the volume declined, and at less than 5% of demand in the period of investigation it is too 
small a share of the market to have displaced the domestic industry, whose own share of demand 
is many times greater. 


57. Price. The Authority records that the landed value of the non-subject imports remained 
below that of the imports from Malaysia throughout the injury period, and that imports from 
Bangladesh in particular entered the Indian market at a markedly low unit value in the period of 
investigation. The Authority does not treat this comparison of aggregate unit values as 
establishing that the non-subject imports set the price in the Indian market. A unit value 
computed over all entries from a source reflects the mix of thicknesses, qualities and grades 
entered from that source, and the record contains no analysis reconciling the product-mix of any 
non-subject source with that of the subject imports or of the domestic like article. A low 
aggregate unit value is therefore as consistent with a thinner or lower-grade mix of entries as it is 
with a genuinely lower price for goods comparable to those under consideration. 


58. Examination. The price injury found in these final findings does not rest upon a 
comparison between one import source and another. It rests upon the comparison between the 
landed value of the subject imports and the domestic industry’s own selling price and cost of 
sales: undercutting in the range of 30-40% in the period of investigation, a selling price that fell 
7% while the cost of sales rose 43%, and a movement from profit into loss. Imports of less than 
5% of demand cannot explain a price effect of that magnitude across the whole market, and the 
volumes concerned declined over the injury period while the injury deepened. The Authority 
therefore determines that the injury suffered by the domestic industry is not attributable to 
the imports from non-subject sources. The Authority records that it has examined this factor 
upon price as well as upon volume. No interested party has, in its post-disclosure comments, 
placed on record any comparison of the non-subject imports with the domestic like article on a 
like-for-like basis of thickness and grade, or any other material showing that the non-subject 
imports set the price in the Indian market. 


N.3 Competition from other Indian producers 


59. Two Indian producers are outside the domestic industry but within the Indian market, 
their sales having been in the range of l0—l5% of demand in the period of investigation, and 
having declined from the level of 2023-24. The Authority determines that competition from 
these producers did not cause the injury suffered by the Applicants, their volumes having 
declined in a growing market in the same period and in the same direction as the price and 
financial parameters of the Applicants, which is consistent with pressure from a common 
external source and not with displacement within the domestic industry. 


[भाग [खण्ड |] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण I7 


N.4 Contraction in demand and pattern of consumption 


60. The Authority notes that the demand for the subject goods increased throughout the 
injury investigation period. The domestic industry has therefore not suffered injury due to a 
contraction in demand. It is further noted that there has been no material change in the pattern of 
consumption of the product under consideration which could have caused injury to the domestic 
industry. 


N.5 Conditions of competition and developments in technology 


6l. The Authority notes that there is no evidence of conditions of competition or trade 
restrictive practices which could have been responsible for the claimed injury to the domestic 
industry, and that there has been no change in technology for the production of the subject goods 
which could have caused such injury. 


N.6 Export performance and productivity of the domestic industry 


62. The injury information examined hereinabove relates only to the performance of the 
domestic industry in terms of its domestic market. Export volumes were in any event small, 
having declined to an index of 58 in the period of investigation (base year = l00) and being a 
small fraction of its domestic sales. Productivity per day increased over the injury period. The 
injury suffered cannot be attributed to the export performance or to the productivity of the 
domestic industry. 


N.7 Conclusion on causal link 


63. The Authority has examined the volume and price effects of the subsidised imports and 
each of the other known factors advanced or apparent upon the record, and has separated and 
distinguished the injurious effects of those other factors, in particular the effect of the domestic 
industry’s own capacity expansion upon its cost of sales, which has not been attributed to the 
subsidised imports. The Authority confirms that there is clear causal link between the injury 
suffered by the domestic industry and imports from Malaysia. 


O. MAGNITUDE OF INJURY MARGIN 


64. The Authority has determined the non-injurious price for the domestic industry in 
accordance with the principles and the methodology uniformly adopted by the Authority for the 
determination of the non-injurious price. The non-injurious price of the product under 
consideration has been determined by adopting the verified information and data relating to the 
cost of production for the period of investigation. For determining the non-injurious price, the 
best utilisation of the raw materials by the domestic industry over the injury period has been 
considered, and the same treatment has been carried out with the utilities. The best utilisation of 
production capacity over the injury period has been considered. It is ensured that no 
extraordinary or non-recurring expenses are charged to the cost of production. A reasonable 
return (pre-tax @ 22%) on average capital employed, that is average net fixed assets plus 
average working capital, for the product under consideration was allowed as pre-tax profit to 
arrive at the non-injurious price in accordance with the said methodology. 


65. The Authority has considered the contention that inefficiencies and expansion- 
related costs should be excluded in determining the non-injurious price. The contention is 


8 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.]] 


well founded in principle and has been given effect. The principles of best utilisation applied to 
raw materials, utilities and production capacity operate to exclude the inefficiency arising from 
under-utilized capacity, and the exclusion of extraordinary and non-recurring expenses operates 
to exclude one-time expansion costs, so that the non-injurious price is not inflated by the 
domestic industry’s own inefficiency. 


66. The landed price for the cooperating exporters has been determined on the basis of the 
data furnished by the exporters. For all the non-cooperating producers/exporters of Malaysia, the 
Authority has determined the landed price on the basis of facts available. The injury margin is 
the amount by which the non-injurious price exceeds the landed price, expressed as a 
percentage of the landed price. A single weighted average non-injurious price has been applied 
to every producer and exporter of Malaysia. No injury margin has been determined in respect of 
Indonesia, the investigation in respect of that country being terminated. 


Producer / Exporter Injury margin (%) Range (%) 
Malaysia
