P. ISSUES OF THE INDIAN INDUSTRY 
P.l Submissions made on behalf of the opposing interested parties 


67. The submissions of the other interested parties with regard to the Indian industry’s 
interest are as follows: 


i. That anti-subsidy duties on the subject goods will significantly raise production costs for 
the downstream industry. 


ul. That higher costs could lead to imports of the ultimate products into India. 


ii. That the impact of the duties is much more than what is indicated by the domestic 
industry. 


P.2 Submissions made on behalf of the Domestic Industry 


68. The submissions of the domestic industry with regard to the Indian industry’s interest are 
as follows: 


i. That in the light of the widening trade deficit it is important to rely more on domestic 
production capacities, and that the imposition of duties would allow conservation of 
foreign exchange favouring the balance of payments account. 


il. That the impact of the imposition of anti-subsidy duty on the subject goods will be 
negligible as compared with the cost of the final product. 


[भाग [खण्ड |] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 9 


ili. That since there are already six players in the market, there would be no monopoly in 
India and users would have enough sources in the domestic market as well. 


iv. That the product under consideration can be imported at fair prices from other sources 
also, and that there would therefore be no adverse impact on users. 


v. That the impact of duties would be minimal in project cost based on the prices prevailing 
in the period of investigation. 


P.3 Examination by the Authority 


69. The Authority notes that the primary objective of anti-subsidy duty is to remedy the 
injury caused to the domestic industry by subsidisation and to restore conditions of fair 
competition in the Indian market. The imposition of anti-subsidy measures is not designed to 
restrict imports; it follows a determination of subsidisation, injury and the causal link between 
the two, and operates to ensure that imports are available at fair and non-subsidised prices. The 
imposition of duty would not affect the availability of the product. 


70. The Authority issued the initiation notification inviting the views of all interested parties, 
including importers, consumers and others, and prescribed a questionnaire for users and 
consumers to provide relevant information. An Economic Interest Questionnaire was also 
prescribed to allow various stakeholders, including the domestic industry, producers/exporters 
and importers, users and consumers, to furnish pertinent information related to the investigation 
including the possible effect of anti-subsidy duty upon their operations. 


7l. The Authority notes that no importer, user or user association filed a questionnaire 
response or any submission in the present investigation. The contentions so recorded have 
accordingly been advanced without any supporting evidence, and no party has furnished any 
analysis, data or working showing how the duty would affect its financial performance or the 
price to the final consumer. The estimate of the domestic industry that the impact upon end 
consumers would be insignificant stands uncontroverted upon the record. 


72. The Authority further notes that six producers operate in the Indian market and that the 
product under consideration is available from non-subject sources, so that the imposition of duty 
would not restrict the availability of the product or competition in the Indian market. 


Q. POST-DISCLOSURE COMMENTS 


Q.l Submissions made on behalf of the opposing interested parties 


73. Post-disclosure comments have been received from Xinyi Energy Smart (Malaysia) Sdn. 
Bhd., Jinjing Technology Malaysia Sdn. Bhd. and Kibing Group (M) Sdn. Bhd., each dated 
24.09.2026, and from other interested parties. The following submissions have been made by 
them: 


a. That the Authority has recorded the responding producers/exporters as cooperating 
parties, and that their individual subsidy margins should be determined on the basis of the 
data furnished in their own questionnaire responses, as examined against their supporting 


20 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.]] 


records during desk verification, without resort to petition-based estimates or residual 
methodology. 


That the subsidy margins determined under certain programmes require correction, the 
computation not being in accordance with the methodology recorded by the Authority; 
that the benefit under the income-tax programmes should be the tax actually foregone and 
not the gross amount of the allowance; and that the amounts paid for natural gas, as 
reflected in the invoices on record, have not been correctly recorded. 


That the Authority has not examined whether the prices actually paid by the respondents 
for natural gas were distorted, and that arm’s-length domestic transactions in Malaysia 
should have been considered before resorting to an out-of-country benchmark. 


That the adoption of an out-of-country benchmark is inconsistent with the approach 
adopted by the Authority in its recent findings concerning Textured Tempered Glass from 
Malaysia. 


That the source and underlying data of the out-of-country benchmark have not been 
adequately disclosed. 


That any out-of-country benchmark must be appropriately adjusted for differences in the 
nature and delivery of gas, transportation, regasification, quality, calorific value, 
contractual terms, taxes and other relevant factors. 


That the Malaysia Industrial Park Directory is a general publication and not a transaction- 
specific benchmark for the land, tenure, location and conditions concerned. 


That the land was not received directly from the Government or a public body, having 
been acquired from an affiliate, and that the finding of financial contribution requires 
reconsideration. 


That the allocation of the land benefit over fifteen years lacks a disclosed basis, and that 
the tenure of the land and the past practice of the Authority warrant a longer allocation 
period. 


That the exchange rates applied do not correspond to those furnished by the respondents 
in their questionnaire responses. 


That the allocation period adopted by the respondents for the exemption from import duty 
on machinery and spare parts is supported by their records and should be retained. 


[भाग [खण्ड |] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 2] 


That the relevant relationships and transactions with related and cross-owned entities 
have been disclosed in the questionnaire responses, that no specific undisclosed 
transaction or benefit has been identified, and that no adverse inference should be drawn. 


. That the Minimum Import Price recently introduced for Clear Float Glass is a material 
change in import conditions, that an additional ad valorem countervailing duty would 
result in over-remediation, and that, in the alternative, the duty should take the form of a 
reference price. 


That the Authority may kindly confirm the proposed termination of the CVD 
investigation in respect of Indonesia under Rule 6(I)(d), since the verified imports from 
Indonesia were below the prescribed 4% threshold and the collective share of developing 
countries individually below 4% also remained below the applicable 9% threshold. 
Consequently, no subsidy margin, injury margin or countervailing duty is warranted 
against Indonesia, and no adverse finding concerning the alleged Indonesian subsidy 
programmes should be recorded. 


That, since no programme-wise determination has been made in respect of Indonesia, the 
Respondent’s rights and submissions concerning the alleged subsidy programmes remain 
fully reserved. In the event the Authority proposes to depart from the proposed 
termination, the Respondent should be provided the complete factual and legal basis, 
programme-wise essential facts, benchmarks and calculations, along with a meaningful 
opportunity to respond before any such finding is incorporated in the Final Findings. 


Q.2 Submissions made on behalf of the Domestic Industry 


74. The domestic industry, in its comments dated 23.09.2026, has made the following 
submissions: 
a. That the Disclosure Statement itself records material deficiencies in the disclosures made 


by the participating Malaysian producers/exporters with respect to their related, affiliated 
and cross-owned entities. It was submitted that the exporters have not furnished complete 
information regarding such entities and their transactions relating to electricity, land, 
finance, inputs, utilities and services. The Domestic Industry contended that such 
information is material for determining the existence, recipient, nature, quantum and 
attribution of countervailable benefits and, therefore, the deficient disclosures cannot form 
the basis for determination of individual subsidy margins without appropriate recourse to 
facts available under Rule 7(8). Reliance was placed upon Japan - DRAMs (Korea), US — 
Countervailing Measures on Certain Products from Korea and Article 2.7 of the SCM 
Agreement. 


22 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.]] 


b. That Jinjing Technology Malaysia Sdn. Bhd. has not disclosed the income-tax exemption 
for a “5+5” year period recorded at page 90 of the Annual Report 2025 of its group, which 
requires examination for determination of the complete countervailable subsidy. 


c. The Domestic Industry has further drawn attention to the United States Department of 
Commerce determination concerning the same product and Malaysian exporters, wherein 
cross-owned entities were identified in the corporate structures of Jinjing Malaysia and 
Xinyi Malaysia. The Domestic Industry has clarified that it does not seek mechanical 
adoption of the subsidy rates determined by the U.S. authority, but submits that the 
determination independently demonstrates the relevance of group-level examination and 
reinforces the need for complete disclosure of related and cross-owned entities in the 
present investigation. 


d. That verification cannot cure a fundamentally incomplete response, as verification is 
intended to verify information disclosed by a respondent and cannot substitute for 
disclosure of entities and transactions which the respondent failed to identify in its 
response. The Domestic Industry has therefore requested that, to the extent the necessary 
information remains undisclosed, the Authority determine the subsidy attributable to the 
exporters on the basis of facts available under Rule 7(8), including appropriate attribution 
of benefits received by relevant related or cross-owned entities. 


e. That the Government of Malaysia has not provided the programme-wise information 
sought by the Authority, including information concerning subsidy programmes 
countervailed by the U.S. authority and the reasons for their alleged non-applicability in 
the present investigation. It has been submitted that assertions of non-applicability, in the 
absence of the underlying information and supporting evidence, cannot substitute for 
information necessary for verification. 


f. With respect to imports from Bangladesh, that the application had placed such imports 
below 3% of total imports at about = 22,000/MT, and that the Authority should re-check 
the share of 5.5% recorded in the Disclosure Statement; and that such imports are 
subject to provisional assessment pursuant to the order dated 9.2.2022 of the Hon’ble 
Delhi High Court in Asahi India Glass Limited v. Union of India & Anr., W.P.(C) 
786/2022, which should be taken into account while examining their volume, price and 
market significance. 


g. That the Authority should confirm existence of material injury and causal link in the 
Final Findings. 


Q.3_Examination by the Authority 


75. The Authority has examined the post-disclosure submissions made by the interested 
parties. The majority of these submissions reiterate arguments already examined and addressed 
in the relevant paragraphs of these final findings. To the extent submissions raise new or specific 
contentions, they are examined below. 


[भाग [खण्ड |] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 23 


On the status of the responding producers/exporters as cooperating parties 


76. The responding producers/exporters filed their questionnaire responses within the time 
allowed, responded to the clarifications sought, and furnished the records against which their 
responses were examined. They have accordingly been treated as cooperating, and individual 
subsidy margins have been determined for each of them on the basis of their own information. 
Where a programme was referred to by an exporter otherwise than in its programme-wise 
answers, the Authority has examined it on the basis of the records on file. 


On the computation of subsidy margins 


77. The Authority has examined the contentions of the responding exporters that the subsidy 
margins require reconsideration on the basis of the exporters’ own computations. The benefit 
amounts, turnover and other figures adopted in the determination are those which emerged upon 
desk verification of each respondent’s supporting records. Where the verified figure differed 
from the self-reported figure, the verified figure has been preferred. On re-examination, the 
Authority has found that the benefits reported by Kibing Group (M) Sdn. Bhd. under the 
Investment Tax Allowance, the Industrial Building Allowance and the Accelerated Capital 
Allowance represented the gross allowance and not the tax foregone, and that the sales tax 
exemption on equipment, being a capital-linked benefit, had been expensed wholly to the period 
of investigation. These benefits have been recomputed as recorded under Programmes 3, 4 and 5, 
and the subsidy margin of Kibing Group (M) Sdn. Bhd. and the residual subsidy margin for 
Malaysia have been revised accordingly. The revision is downward and arises from the 
respondent’s own submissions. Save as aforesaid, the subsidy margins as determined are 
maintained. 


On the natural gas benchmark 


78. The Authority has considered the submissions that (a) the Authority has not examined 
whether the prices paid by the individual respondents are distorted; (b) observable arm’s-length 
domestic transactions in Malaysia should have been considered before resorting to an out-of- 
country benchmark; (c) the adoption of an Indonesian benchmark is inconsistent with the 
Authority’s finding dated 03.03.2026 in the anti-subsidy investigation concerning imports of 
Textured Tempered Glass from Malaysia; and (d) the source and underlying data for the 
benchmark have not been adequately disclosed. 

79. On point (a), the finding in paragraphs 66 to 68 is not a finding that the individual 
respondent’s transaction was distorted; it is a finding that the Malaysian domestic reference price 
itself — being the administered price fixed under the very framework alleged to confer the 
subsidy, in a market in which a wholly State-owned corporation is the sole aggregator of 
domestic supply — cannot serve as a measure of the adequacy of remuneration. The inquiry 


[24 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.]] 


under Article l4(d) is directed to the adequacy of the remuneration, not to the terms of the 
individual buyer’s contract. 


80. On point (b), no respondent has placed on record any arm’s-length, non-administered 
domestic gas price prevailing in Malaysia during the period of investigation which could have 
served as an in-country benchmark. No alternative in-country price has been identified. 


8l. On point (c), each investigation proceeds upon its own record, its own product under 
consideration and its own period of investigation. The determination in the Textured Tempered 
Glass investigation was made upon a different record and does not bind the present investigation. 


82. On point (9), the benchmark prices were drawn from the published tariff schedules and 
publicly available gas-price data for the non-administered segment of the Indonesian gas market, 
as placed on the record. The month-wise series, the price category and the figures have been 
disclosed in the Disclosure Statement and in these final findings. 


83. As regards the submission that the Indonesian benchmark should be subject to 
adjustments, the Authority notes that the benchmark adopted for April 2024 was the non- 
administered pipeline price for the lowest published consumer category, being the figure least 
adverse to the respondents. For the remaining months, the regasified LNG price has been taken. 
The respondents have not placed on record any quantification of the adjustments they seek. In 
the absence of quantified adjustments, no adjustment has been made. 


On the provision of land at LTAR 

84. On the benchmark, no respondent furnished a guideline value or contended that a 
contemporaneous comparable transaction was available. The Authority has relied upon the 
Malaysia Industrial Park Directory, applying the lower end of the reported range. No alternative 
benchmark has been placed on the record. The benchmark is maintained. 


85. On the submission that the land was acquired from a cross-owned affiliate, the Authority 
reiterates the finding in paragraph 49(iii): the interposition of a cross-owned affiliate does not 
defeat the finding of financial contribution. 


86. On the allocation period, the benefit has been allocated over fifteen years under Annexure 
IV, the allocation period being referable to the useful life of the asset employed in production 
and not to the duration of the title. As regards the Aluminium Primary Foundry Alloy Ingot 
investigation, that determination was made upon a different record and does not bind the present 
investigation. 


On the exchange rate 
87. The Authority has applied the exchange rate drawn from each respondent’s own books 
and supporting records as verified during desk verification. The verified rates are confirmed. 


[भाग [खण्ड |] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 25 


On the allocation period for import duty exemption on machinery and spare parts 


88. Having examined the respondents’ submissions, the Authority notes that the exemption 
from import duty on machinery relates to capital assets employed in production over a number of 
years, and is accordingly a non-recurring benefit. Consistently with the methodology recorded in 
Section G and with the treatment of the exemption on equipment in the case of Kibing Group 
(M) Sdn. Bhd., the benefit has been allocated over the average useful life of fifteen years. The 
allocation period of twelve years proposed by the respondents is accordingly not adopted. 


On related and cross-owned entities 


89. Subsidies received by a cross-owned entity are attributable to a respondent only where 
that entity supplies an input primarily dedicated to the production of the subject goods, or is a 
holding company or a producer of the subject goods, and has itself received a financial 
contribution from the Government of Malaysia. The Authority has applied that test to each entity 
named by the domestic industry. Jinjing: its own records, including its income-tax return, 
disclose no purchase of inputs from any related entity in Malaysia, and its related-party 
transactions are with entities outside Malaysia or with private entities, none of which involves a 
financial contribution by the Government of Malaysia. Xinyi: Xinyi has disclosed purchases of a 
material from a related entity and transportation services from Xin Yun Logistics (Malaysia) 
Sdn. Bhd.; transportation services are not an input into the subject goods, and no programme has 
been identified under which the related supplier received a financial contribution in respect of the 
material supplied. Other entities named: as regards Xinyi Photovoltaic (Malaysia) Co., Ltd., CS 
Eco Glass (M) Sdn. Bhd. and Malaysia Kibing Energy Saving Glass Co., Ltd., no material has 
been placed on record showing that any of them supplied an input to the respondent concerned in 
the period of investigation or received a financial contribution from the Government of Malaysia 
attributable to the subject goods. The contention that the responses be rejected in their entirety is 
accordingly not accepted. The concern that all benefits be examined is accepted; the exemption 
held by Jinjing has been examined as Programme 7. 


On Jinjing’s income-tax exemption and U.S. Commerce findings 


90. Jinjing’s income-tax exemption. The contention of the domestic industry has been 
examined. Jinjing’s own records establish that it held an exemption of l00 per cent of its 
statutory income under the Northern Corridor Economic Region during the period of 
investigation, and the programme has been examined as Programme 7. Jinjing itself referred to 
the exemption in its questionnaire response and explained that no benefit had been received 
under it. Its tax computation for the year of assessment falling within the period of investigation 
shows that no statutory income was exempted and no tax was foregone. For the reasons recorded 
at Programme 7, no amount has been countervailed under it. 


26 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.]] 


9!. U.S. Commerce findings. The U.S. Department of Commerce identified Jinjing Silicon 
Technology Sdn. Bhd. and Xin Yun Logistics (Malaysia) Sdn. Bhd. as cross-owned with Jinjing 
and Xinyi respectively. The Authority has examined whether these entities supplied inputs to the 
respondents. Jinjing’s own records disclose no purchase from any related entity in Malaysia, and 
no transaction with Jinjing Silicon Technology Sdn. Bhd. Xin Yun Logistics provides 
transportation services, which is not an input into the subject goods. The U.S. determination rests 
upon a different period and record, and establishes cross-ownership but not the supply of any 
input in the present period of investigation. The contention is examined and not accepted. 


On non-submission of programme-wise information by the Government of Malaysia 


92. The Authority has assessed the relevant programmes on the basis of the information and 
explanations available on record. Where information was not furnished, the Authority has 
proceeded on the basis of facts available in accordance with the Rules. 


On the import data from Bangladesh 


93. The share of imports from Bangladesh has been determined from the transaction-wise 
import data of DG Systems for the product under consideration, being 9,482 MT out of total 
imports of 3,78,226 MT, or 5.5] per cent. The figure in the application was an estimate made at 
the stage of initiation on the information then reasonably available; the domestic industry has not 
placed on record any transaction-level data establishing a different volume, and the primary data 
prevail. The provisional assessment pursuant to the order of the Hon’ble Delhi High Court 
affects the duty liability on those imports, not the fact or the volume of their import for home 
consumption, and they are accordingly counted in total imports. The contention is examined and 
not accepted. 


On the Minimum Import Price notification 


94. The MIP is a trade-policy measure issued under the Foreign Trade (Development and 
Regulation) Act, 992, distinct in legal basis, purpose, duration and administering authority from 
a countervailing duty under Section 9 of the Customs Tariff Act, 975. The two measures 
operate independently. The injury determination and subsidy margins have been established 
upon verified data for the period of investigation. The MIP, having been notified after the period 
of investigation, does not affect the determination of subsidisation or injury. The existence of a 
separate trade-policy measure of limited duration does not remove the need for a measure to 
offset the subsidisation and injury found where subsidisation, injury and causal link have been 
established. 


95. The submission that the duty should take the form of a reference price is not accepted. 
The ad valorem form is consistently adopted by the Authority in countervailing duty proceedings 
and is appropriate in the present case. 


[भाग [खण्ड |] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 27 


On material injury and causal link 


96. The Authority confirms material injury to the domestic industry on account of subsidised 
imports from Malaysia, as determined on the basis of verified import data, price effects, injury 
parameters and other relevant factors on record. 


On the termination of the investigation in respect of Indonesia 


97. The volume of imports from Indonesia is below the four per cent threshold in the period 
of investigation and in each year of the injury period. The collective share of developing 
countries individually below four per cent is below nine per cent. No interested party placed 
evidence of potential volume above the threshold on the record. The termination under Rule 
6()(d) is confirmed. No subsidy margin, injury margin or countervailing duty is determined or 
recommended in respect of Indonesia, and no programme-wise finding has been made. The 
Indonesian respondent’s rights concerning the alleged programmes are noted as reserved. 


R. CONCLUSION 


98. Having regard to the contentions raised, information provided, and submissions made by 
the interested parties and facts available before the Authority, as recorded in the above finding, 
and on the basis of above analysis of the subsidisation, injury and causal link to the domestic 
industry, the Authority concludes as follows: 
i. The Applicants constitute the domestic industry within the meaning of Rule 2(b) of 
the Rules and the application satisfies the criteria of standing. 


ii. The product under consideration is Clear Float Glass of nominal thickness 4 mm to 
2 mm as per BIS 4900:2000, and the subject goods produced by the domestic 
industry are a like article thereto. 


iii. Indonesia and Malaysia are each a developing country for the purposes of Rule 
6() of the Rules. The volume of the subsidised imports from Indonesia was 
3.40% of the total imports of the like article into India in the period of 
investigation, which is below the threshold of four per cent, and the collective share 
of all developing countries individually below four per cent is 5.897%, which is 
below the threshold of nine per cent. The investigation in respect of Indonesia is 
accordingly terminated under Rule !6(.)(d), and no subsidy margin, injury margin 
or duty is recommended in respect of that country. 


iv. The volume of imports from Malaysia is above the threshold prescribed under Rule 
6()(d), and the investigation continues in respect of that country. 


v.  Countervailable subsidies were availed by the participating producers and exporters 
of Malaysia during the period of investigation, the subsidy margins determined 
being .6% (range l0—20%) in respect of Xinyi Energy Smart (Malaysia) Sdn. 
Bhd., .44% (range l0—20%) in respect of Kibing Group (M) Sdn. Bhd. and 
8.58% (range 0-l0%) in respect of Jinjing Technology Malaysia Sdn. Bhd., with a 
residual margin of 5.66% (range 0—20%) for Malaysia 


28 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.!] 


vi. The domestic industry has suffered injury during the period of investigation in the 
form of significant price undercutting, price depression and price suppression. The 
injury so suffered is material, being reflected in losses, cash losses, negative return 
on investment, accumulation of inventories and declining capacity utilisation. 


vii. The injury margins determined in respect of the producers and exporters of 
Malaysia are as set out in Section O above, and in every case the subsidy margin is 
lower than the injury margin, so that the duty recommended upon application of the 
lesser duty rule is equal to the subsidy margin. 


viii. The material injury suffered by the domestic industry has been caused by the 
subsidised imports of the subject goods from Malaysia. The injurious effects of the 
other known factors, in particular the effect of the capacity expansion of the 
domestic industry upon its cost of sales, have been separated and distinguished and 
have not been attributed to the subsidised imports. 


ix. The Authority is satisfied that the imposition of a definitive countervailing duty on 
imports of the subject goods originating in or exported from Malaysia is warranted 
to offset the subsidisation and the injury caused thereby, in the form and manner set 
out in Section S below. 


S. RECOMMENDATIONS 


99. The Authority notes that the investigation was initiated and notified to all interested 
parties including Government of Malaysia and adequate opportunity was given to provide 
information/evidence on the aspect of subsidization, injury and causal links in favour or against 
thereof. Having initiated and conducted the investigation into subsidization, injury and causal 
links in terms of the Rules laid down and having established positive subsidy margin as well as 
material injury to the domestic industry caused by such subsidized imports, the Authority is of 
the view that imposition of definitive countervailing duty is required to offset subsidization and 
injury. Therefore, the Authority considers it necessary to recommend imposition of definitive 
countervailing duty on the imports of the subject goods from Malaysia in the form and manner 
described hereunder. 


200. In accordance with Rule l9 of the Rules, and having regard to the lesser duty rule 
followed, the Designated Authority hereby recommends the imposition of an anti-subsidy duty 
equal to the lesser of the margin of subsidy and the margin of injury on all imports of the subject 
goods originating in or exported from Malaysia so as to eliminate the material injury caused to 
the domestic industry. 


Accordingly, under the powers conferred by the aforementioned provision, the Authority 
considers it necessary to recommend, and does hereby recommend, the imposition of a definitive 
anti-subsidy duty on the imports of the subject goods from Malaysia for a period of five (5) years 
from the date of publication of the official notification by the Central Government, at the rates 
specified in Column 7 of the duty table appended below

T. FURTHER PROCEDURE 


202. An appeal against the order of the Central Government arising out of this final finding 
shall lie before the Customs, Excise and Service Tax Appellate Tribunal in accordance with 
Section 9C of the Customs Tariff Act, 975.
